### Relational Graph Transformer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10960v1)
- **Authors**: Vijay Prakash Dwivedi, Sri Jaladi, Yangyi Shen, Federico López, Charilaos I. Kanatsoulis, Rishi Puri, Matthias Fey, Jure Leskovec
- **Abstract**: Relational Deep Learning (RDL) is a promising approach for building state-of-the-art predictive models on multi-table relational data by representing it as a heterogeneous temporal graph. However, commonly used Graph Neural Network models suffer from fundamental limitations in capturing complex structural patterns and long-range dependencies that are inherent in relational data. While Graph Transformers have emerged as powerful alternatives to GNNs on general graphs, applying them to relational entity graphs presents unique challenges: (i) Traditional positional encodings fail to generalize to massive, heterogeneous graphs; (ii) existing architectures cannot model the temporal dynamics and schema constraints of relational data; (iii) existing tokenization schemes lose critical structural information. Here we introduce the Relational Graph Transformer (RelGT), the first graph transformer architecture designed specifically for relational tables. RelGT employs a novel multi-element tokenization strategy that decomposes each node into five components (features, type, hop distance, time, and local structure), enabling efficient encoding of heterogeneity, temporality, and topology without expensive precomputation. Our architecture combines local attention over sampled subgraphs with global attention to learnable centroids, incorporating both local and database-wide representations. Across 21 tasks from the RelBench benchmark, RelGT consistently matches or outperforms GNN baselines by up to 18%, establishing Graph Transformers as a powerful architecture for Relational Deep Learning.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper and will provide a summary, followed by a rigorous and critical evaluation of its novelty and significance.

**Paper Summary:**

The paper introduces RELGT (Relational Graph Transformer), a novel graph transformer architecture specifically designed for relational deep learning (RDL) on multi-table relational data. It addresses limitations of standard GNNs and existing Graph Transformers when applied to relational entity graphs (REGs). RELGT employs a multi-element tokenization strategy, decomposing each node into five components: features, type, hop distance, time, and local structure. This allows efficient encoding of heterogeneity, temporality, and topology without expensive precomputation. The architecture combines local attention over sampled subgraphs with global attention to learnable centroids.  Experiments on 21 tasks from the RelBench benchmark show RELGT consistently matches or outperforms GNN baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Strength:** RELGT's core novelty lies in its tailored design for RDL.  The multi-element tokenization strategy is a significant departure from traditional positional encodings in Graph Transformers, making it suitable for heterogeneous, temporal, and large-scale relational data. The combination of local subgraph attention with global centroid attention is also a novel architectural choice aimed at balancing fine-grained structural modeling with database-wide patterns.
    *   **Weakness:** While the combination of techniques is novel, individual components draw inspiration from existing graph transformer architectures (local/global attention), and tabular data processing approaches, and it uses ideas that are well-known in the GNN literature. The "stochastic initialization" of node features in the subgraph GNN component, while effective, is a somewhat relaxed version of existing methods like learnable PEs, reducing its novelty somewhat.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Strength:** The paper addresses a clear need in the field of RDL. RDL is increasingly important for enterprise data analysis, but existing methods (primarily GNNs) struggle with the specific characteristics of relational data.  RELGT demonstrably improves performance on RelBench, a well-established benchmark for RDL, suggesting real-world applicability.  The model is competitive without significantly increasing computational complexity when compared to GNNs.
    *   **Weakness:** The empirical evaluation focuses solely on RelBench. While comprehensive, expanding the evaluation to other relational datasets or real-world applications would strengthen the paper's claims about generalizability and impact. While RELGT consistently outperforms GNN baselines, the gains aren't always substantial on all datasets or tasks. The ablation studies are useful, but they also reveal task-dependent behavior, suggesting that careful tuning might be necessary for optimal performance in specific scenarios. The comparison with HGT is not entirely fair. HGT was not specifically designed for the relational database setting, and it doesn't incorporate the same multi-element tokenization strategy as RELGT.

*   **Rigorousness:**

    *   **Strength:** The paper provides detailed descriptions of the architecture, tokenization process, and experimental setup. Ablation studies are conducted to evaluate the contribution of individual components. The benchmark is well-established.
    *   **Weakness:** The hyperparameter tuning isn't exhaustive, so it's difficult to assess how much the performance could be improved with more extensive optimization. The discussion of task-specific dependencies and possible areas of improvements is useful but also highlights that there is room for further research to generalize and improve RELGT's performance.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper is likely to influence research in RDL and graph transformers. It demonstrates the potential of graph transformers for relational data and provides a concrete architecture that can be used as a starting point for future work. The multi-element tokenization strategy could inspire new encoding methods for heterogeneous graphs in general.

**Overall Assessment:**

RELGT represents a valuable contribution to the field of relational deep learning. The architecture's design, specifically targeting the challenges of relational data, and the promising empirical results justify its publication. The paper addresses an important problem and offers a viable solution. However, the incremental nature of some components, the limited generalizability, and the need for more exhaustive tuning prevent it from being considered a truly groundbreaking contribution.

### MPS-Prover: Advancing Stepwise Theorem Proving by Multi-Perspective Search and Data Curation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10962v1)
- **Authors**: Zhenwen Liang, Linfeng Song, Yang Li, Tao Yang, Feng Zhang, Haitao Mi, Dong Yu
- **Abstract**: Automated Theorem Proving (ATP) in formal languages remains a formidable challenge in AI, demanding rigorous logical deduction and navigating vast search spaces. While large language models (LLMs) have shown promising performance, existing stepwise provers often suffer from biased search guidance, leading to inefficiencies and suboptimal proof strategies. This paper introduces the Multi-Perspective Search Prover (MPS-Prover), a novel stepwise ATP system designed to overcome these limitations. MPS-Prover incorporates two key innovations: a highly effective post-training data curation strategy that prunes approximately 40% of redundant training data without sacrificing performance, and a multi-perspective tree search mechanism. This search integrates a learned critic model with strategically designed heuristic rules to diversify tactic selection, prevent getting trapped in unproductive states, and enhance search robustness. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that MPS-Prover achieves state-of-the-art performance on multiple challenging benchmarks, including miniF2F and ProofNet, outperforming prior 7B parameter models. Furthermore, our analyses reveal that MPS-Prover generates significantly shorter and more diverse proofs compared to existing stepwise and whole-proof methods, highlighting its efficiency and efficacy. Our work advances the capabilities of LLM-based formal reasoning and offers a robust framework and a comprehensive analysis for developing more powerful theorem provers.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the MPS-Prover paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MPS-Prover (Multi-Perspective Search Prover), a novel approach to automated theorem proving that enhances stepwise proving through a combination of post-training data curation and a multi-perspective tree search. The data curation strategy filters out redundant training data to improve model accuracy, while the multi-perspective search incorporates heuristic critiques to diversify tactic selection and prevent getting trapped in unproductive states. The authors demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on miniF2F and ProofNet benchmarks compared to previous 7B models. Additionally, they show that MPS-Prover generates shorter and more diverse proofs than existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of data curation *and* multi-perspective search is a key point of novelty. The data curation strategy appears well-considered and practically useful. Filtering short proofs and ineffective tactics is a relatively simple, yet effective method to improve model training. The multi-perspective search, which incorporates heuristic critiques, builds upon BFS techniques. While BFS is not new, strategically designing heuristics tailored to the limitations of learned critics in theorem proving (repetitive tactics, unprovable states, ineffective applications) is a significant contribution. It addresses a crucial problem of bias and local optima in LLM-based provers.  The paper successfully demonstrates the effectiveness of the curated dataset with the performance results.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from achieving state-of-the-art performance on multiple competitive benchmarks, especially ProofNet (which is harder than miniF2F). Surpassing previous step-wise provers like BFS-Prover in miniF2F (both in peak and constrained budget performance) and more importantly surpassing previous best models of its size in ProofNet (including CoT) demonstrates a significant advance. The detailed analysis of proof length and diversity adds further value, showcasing *how* MPS-Prover achieves its performance gains. Generating shorter proofs is particularly valuable in the context of formal verification. The ablation studies provide a solid understanding of the contribution of each component, validating the design choices. The case studies highlight a core advantage of the methodology.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and well-motivated approach.
    *   Effective combination of data curation and multi-perspective search.
    *   State-of-the-art performance on challenging benchmarks.
    *   Comprehensive ablation studies and detailed analysis of proof characteristics.
    *   Detailed case studies give a great insight into the methodology.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The individual heuristic rules in the multi-perspective search, while effective, are somewhat hand-crafted. While the paper adequately justifies their design, a more automated or learned approach to heuristic design could potentially further enhance the system's robustness.
    *   The paper acknowledges a limitation of step-wise provers when dealing with tactics that require nested sub-proofs.  While it correctly identifies this as a direction for future work, the scope of its impact is not entirely quantified.
    *   The reliance on expert iteration, while standard in ATP research, means that the dataset is constrained by problems solved under human effort. A better exploration for a wider range of problems might enhance future iterations.
    *   While MPS-Prover significantly reduces proof length relative to previous methods, it remains longer than a human-derived proof. This means that this method has room for enhancement on proof quality.

*   **Potential Influence:**  This paper has the potential to significantly influence the development of future LLM-based theorem provers. The data curation strategy can be readily adopted by other researchers. The multi-perspective search offers a robust framework for addressing the biases and limitations of learned critic models. Hybrid approaches combining the strengths of stepwise and whole-proof methods is a promising avenue for future exploration, as suggested in the paper. The work highlights the importance of careful heuristic design and analysis in building effective automated reasoning systems.

*   **Justification for Score:** MPS-Prover represents a solid step forward in automated theorem proving. While relying on some hand-crafted heuristics and acknowledging limitations in handling nested sub-proofs, it demonstrates significant performance gains, generates shorter and more diverse proofs, and offers a valuable framework for addressing the challenges of LLM-based provers. The impact is further strengthened by the extensive experiments and analyses.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### Group-in-Group Policy Optimization for LLM Agent Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10978v1)
- **Authors**: Lang Feng, Zhenghai Xue, Tingcong Liu, Bo An
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in group-based reinforcement learning (RL) have driven frontier large language models (LLMs) in single-turn tasks like mathematical reasoning. However, their scalability to long-horizon LLM agent training remains limited. Unlike static tasks, agent-environment interactions unfold over many steps and often yield sparse or delayed rewards, making credit assignment across individual steps significantly more challenging. In this work, we propose Group-in-Group Policy Optimization (GiGPO), a novel RL algorithm that achieves fine-grained credit assignment for LLM agents while preserving the appealing properties of group-based RL: critic-free, low memory, and stable convergence. GiGPO introduces a two-level structure for estimating relative advantage: (i) At the episode-level, GiGPO computes macro relative advantages based on groups of complete trajectories; (ii) At the step-level, GiGPO introduces an anchor state grouping mechanism that retroactively constructs step-level groups by identifying repeated environment states across trajectories. Actions stemming from the same state are grouped together, enabling micro relative advantage estimation. This hierarchical structure effectively captures both global trajectory quality and local step effectiveness without relying on auxiliary models or additional rollouts. We evaluate GiGPO on two challenging agent benchmarks, ALFWorld and WebShop, using Qwen2.5-1.5B-Instruct and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct. Crucially, GiGPO delivers fine-grained per-step credit signals and achieves performance gains of > 12\% on ALFWorld and > 9\% on WebShop over the GRPO baseline: all while maintaining the same GPU memory overhead, identical LLM rollout, and incurring little to no additional time cost.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Group-in-Group Policy Optimization (GiGPO), a novel reinforcement learning (RL) algorithm specifically designed for training large language model (LLM) agents in long-horizon tasks. GiGPO addresses the challenge of fine-grained credit assignment in settings where rewards are sparse and delayed. It achieves this by introducing a two-level structure for estimating relative advantage: (1) a macro-level that considers complete trajectories (episodes) and (2) a micro-level that dynamically constructs step-level groups based on "anchor states" (repeated environment states). This avoids the cost of extra rollouts for each step. The algorithm is critic-free (no separate value function approximator) and aims to maintain the favorable properties of group-based RL (low memory, stable convergence) while enhancing credit assignment. The authors evaluate GiGPO on the ALFWorld and WebShop benchmarks, demonstrating improved performance over baselines like GRPO and PPO using Qwen2.5 LLMs.  They demonstrate the approach's benefits without significant computational overhead.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the anchor state grouping mechanism for step-level credit assignment within a group-based RL framework.  While group-based RL has been explored before, the dynamic creation of groups based on recurring environment states to refine per-step advantage estimation is a valuable and novel addition. This allows localized advantage estimation without the costly additional rollouts, enabling finer control over training over longer horizons.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge in applying RL to train LLM agents for complex tasks: effective credit assignment in long-horizon settings with sparse rewards. The experimental results clearly demonstrate that GiGPO outperforms existing group-based RL algorithms and other baselines on established benchmarks. The >12% and >9% improvements over GRPO in ALFWorld and WebShop, respectively, are significant. This suggests that GiGPO can contribute to more effective and efficient training of LLM agents for real-world applications.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of long-horizon LLM agent training and limitations of existing methods.
    *   **Novel Method:** GiGPO introduces a technically sound and intuitive approach to fine-grained credit assignment.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results are compelling and demonstrate the effectiveness of GiGPO across different benchmarks and LLMs. Ablation studies support the claims about the importance of both episode and step-level advantages. Analysis of step level group sizes lends further credence to the method.
    *   **Computational Efficiency:** The method's low computational overhead (shown to be only <0.002% more than GRPO) is a significant advantage for scalability.
    *   **Reproducibility:**  The authors provide code and detailed training setups for reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on State Matching:** The anchor state grouping relies on exact state matching.  While this approach works well in the tested environments, the paper acknowledges this limitation and suggests embedding-based or approximate matching as future work. In highly complex environments, this exact matching might become a bottleneck. Addressing this would broaden the method's applicability.
    *   **Hyperparameter Tuning:** While the weighting coefficient *w* is fixed at 1, the paper could benefit from providing guidance on how to choose this parameter in different scenarios, and if varying it from 1 yields meaningfully different behaviors or outcomes. The statement that it's fixed "without further tuning" suggests that exploration of alternative values was limited.
    *   **Generality of "Anchor States":** The paper convincingly demonstrates benefits in Webshop and ALFWorld, however, it would be interesting to explore how well 'anchor states' generalize to tasks with less visual recurrence and more abstract symbolic states.

*   **Potential Influence:** GiGPO offers a promising approach for training LLM agents in complex, long-horizon tasks. The idea of dynamically constructing step-level groups for refined credit assignment could influence future research in RL and LLM agent training.  The critic-free nature of GiGPO is also beneficial for scalability and stability, making it attractive for training large models.

**Justification for Score:**

Based on the above analysis, I assign a score of **8**.

*   The paper presents a novel and technically sound approach (anchor state grouping) to address a key challenge in training LLM agents.
*   The experimental results are compelling and demonstrate significant improvements over strong baselines.
*   The method is computationally efficient and maintains the desirable properties of group-based RL.
*   The paper acknowledges the limitations of exact state matching and suggests future research directions. However, the reliance on exact state matching is a potential bottleneck that needs further research to fully unlock the method's potential.
*   The paper can influence future research in RL and LLM agent training, particularly in the area of credit assignment.
Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Rethinking the Role of Prompting Strategies in LLM Test-Time Scaling: A Perspective of Probability Theory
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10981v1)
- **Authors**: Yexiang Liu, Zekun Li, Zhi Fang, Nan Xu, Ran He, Tieniu Tan
- **Abstract**: Recently, scaling test-time compute on Large Language Models (LLM) has garnered wide attention. However, there has been limited investigation of how various reasoning prompting strategies perform as scaling. In this paper, we focus on a standard and realistic scaling setting: majority voting. We systematically conduct experiments on 6 LLMs $\times$ 8 prompting strategies $\times$ 6 benchmarks. Experiment results consistently show that as the sampling time and computational overhead increase, complicated prompting strategies with superior initial performance gradually fall behind simple Chain-of-Thought. We analyze this phenomenon and provide theoretical proofs. Additionally, we propose a method according to probability theory to quickly and accurately predict the scaling performance and select the best strategy under large sampling times without extra resource-intensive inference in practice. It can serve as the test-time scaling law for majority voting. Furthermore, we introduce two ways derived from our theoretical analysis to significantly improve the scaling performance. We hope that our research can promote to re-examine the role of complicated prompting, unleash the potential of simple prompting strategies, and provide new insights for enhancing test-time scaling performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper re-examines the role of prompting strategies in Large Language Model (LLM) test-time scaling, specifically focusing on majority voting. Through comprehensive experiments across multiple LLMs, prompting strategies, and benchmarks, the authors consistently find that simple Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting outperforms more complex prompting methods as the test-time compute budget increases. They provide theoretical analysis and empirical evidence to explain this phenomenon, attributing it to CoT's relative advantage in handling "easy" vs. "hard" questions and its robustness to incorrect answers. Furthermore, they introduce a probability theory-based method for quickly predicting scaling performance and dynamically adapting prompting strategies based on question difficulty, ultimately improving performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's central claim, that simple CoT overtakes more complex prompting strategies with increased test-time compute, is a significant and somewhat counter-intuitive observation. While previous work has explored test-time scaling, this paper delves specifically into the nuanced interactions between prompting strategy complexity and scaling. The theoretical analysis, although building on existing concepts, is novel in its application to this specific context and provides a formal explanation for the observed empirical trends. The proposed method for predicting scaling performance based on probability theory is also a valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have important implications for the field. It challenges the prevailing emphasis on developing increasingly complex prompting methods, suggesting that simpler approaches may be more effective when coupled with sufficient compute. This can lead to more efficient utilization of resources and potentially lower the barrier to entry for leveraging LLMs. The proposed method for predicting scaling performance has significant practical value, enabling practitioners to make informed decisions about resource allocation and prompting strategy selection.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper presents a thorough empirical evaluation, covering a wide range of LLMs, prompting strategies, and benchmarks.
    *   **Theoretical Analysis:** The theoretical framework provides a solid foundation for understanding the observed phenomena and makes the paper more than just an empirical study.
    *   **Practical Contributions:** The proposed method for predicting scaling performance is immediately applicable and can help practitioners optimize their LLM deployments.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the concepts and findings.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Scaling Methods:** The paper focuses primarily on majority voting as the test-time scaling technique. While this is a common and effective approach, it would be valuable to explore how the findings generalize to other scaling methods, such as tree search or iterative refinement.
    *   **Reliance on Oracle Information:** While the proposed methods for adapting prompting strategies improve performance, they often rely on "oracle" information (i.e., perfect knowledge of question difficulty or the optimal prompting strategy). Further research is needed to develop more practical, real-world approaches for estimating these quantities.
    *   **Limited generalization across datasets:** While results demonstrate benefit across many datasets, there is not a thorough analysis on why some datasets result in different prompting dominance at large scale. This makes it harder to generalize beyond the specific examples used.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work is likely to influence the future direction of research on LLM prompting. It encourages a more nuanced and computationally-aware approach to prompting strategy selection, moving beyond simply seeking the highest initial performance. The paper's findings may also inspire new research on adaptive prompting techniques that can dynamically adjust the prompting strategy based on question difficulty and resource constraints.

*   **Score Justification:** The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM prompting by challenging the conventional wisdom and providing a more nuanced understanding of the interaction between prompting strategy complexity and test-time scaling. While there are limitations, the paper's findings have important implications for practitioners and future research directions.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### ReaCritic: Large Reasoning Transformer-based DRL Critic-model Scaling For Heterogeneous Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10992v1)
- **Authors**: Feiran You, Hongyang Du
- **Abstract**: Heterogeneous Networks (HetNets) pose critical challenges for intelligent management due to the diverse user requirements and time-varying wireless conditions. These factors introduce significant decision complexity, which limits the adaptability of existing Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) methods. In many DRL algorithms, especially those involving value-based or actor-critic structures, the critic component plays a key role in guiding policy learning by estimating value functions. However, conventional critic models often use shallow architectures that map observations directly to scalar estimates, limiting their ability to handle multi-task complexity. In contrast, recent progress in inference-time scaling of Large Language Models (LLMs) has shown that generating intermediate reasoning steps can significantly improve decision quality. Motivated by this, we propose ReaCritic, a large reasoning transformer-based criticmodel scaling scheme that brings reasoning ability into DRL. ReaCritic performs horizontal reasoning over parallel state-action inputs and vertical reasoning through deep transformer stacks. It is compatible with a broad range of value-based and actor-critic DRL algorithms and enhances generalization in dynamic wireless environments. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ReaCritic improves convergence speed and final performance across various HetNet settings and standard OpenAI Gym control tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "ReaCritic: Large Reasoning Transformer-based DRL Critic-model Scaling For Heterogeneous Networks" proposes a novel Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) critic architecture called ReaCritic. ReaCritic aims to improve the scalability and generalization of DRL in complex, dynamic Heterogeneous Networks (HetNets).  The key idea is to incorporate reasoning capabilities into the critic network, drawing inspiration from Large Language Models (LLMs). ReaCritic employs a two-dimensional reasoning process: Horizontal Reasoning (HRea), which expands the state-action space with multiple token embeddings to simulate different perspectives, and Vertical Reasoning (VRea), which uses stacked Transformer blocks for hierarchical value representation. The authors demonstrate that ReaCritic improves convergence speed, policy stability, and adaptability compared to standard MLP-based critics and other DRL baselines in a simulated HetNet environment and standard OpenAI Gym control tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's primary novelty lies in the adaptation of LLM-inspired architectural principles, specifically reasoning, to the critic component of DRL, and its integration with HetNet resource management.  The concept of introducing horizontal and vertical reasoning through transformer blocks into the critic network is unique, particularly within the context of DRL for wireless resource allocation. While transformer-based DRL methods exist, applying this specifically to the *critic* and emphasizing reasoning is the distinguishing factor. The exploration of diverse hypothetical trajectories in HRea and hierarchical feature extraction in VRea contribute to the uniqueness of the approach.

**Significance:**

The paper addresses a critical problem: the difficulty of applying DRL to complex, dynamic HetNets due to the limitations of traditional critic models. The demonstrated improvements in convergence, stability, and adaptability suggest that ReaCritic has the potential to significantly enhance DRL performance in such environments. The modular design allowing for integration with different DRL algorithms further adds to the significance by making it applicable to a broad range of existing DRL methods. The experimental results in both the custom HetNet environment *and* OpenAI Gym environments provide convincing evidence for the general applicability and potential impact of the approach.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of DRL in HetNets and the limitations of existing approaches.
*   **Well-Motivated Design:** The ReaCritic architecture is well-motivated by the success of reasoning in LLMs and the specific requirements of DRL in dynamic environments.
*   **Thorough Evaluation:** The experiments are extensive and cover a range of scenarios, including varying user densities in the HetNet environment and standard control tasks.
*   **Strong Results:** The results consistently demonstrate the benefits of ReaCritic over standard DRL baselines and MLP-based critics.
*   **Ablation Study:** The inclusion of an ablation study further strengthens the paper by demonstrating the contribution of noise to the design.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Complexity:** While mentioned, a more detailed analysis of the computational overhead introduced by ReaCritic (compared to standard critics) would be valuable. It is important to analyze the increased computational cost, considering real-world hardware constraints.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** While results are strong, the performance can depend on the correct HRea and VRea settings as shown in the ablation study (Fig. 5 & Fig. 6). This sensitivity to hyperparameter choice may make it more complex to deploy and require more tuning.
*   **Limited Real-World Validation:** The experiments are conducted primarily in simulation. Real-world deployments often introduce unforeseen complexities that simulations might not capture. Experimental validation of ReaCritic in a real-world testbed would significantly strengthen the paper.
*   **LLM-Comparisons**: Although inspired by LLMs, there is very limited analytical discussion how the learned "reasoning steps" in the critic can be understood and interpreted, in analogy to chain of thought reasoning.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-motivated architecture with strong experimental results. ReaCritic demonstrably improves DRL performance in complex, dynamic environments. However, the computational complexity needs further analysis, real-world validation is lacking, and the dependency on hyperparameter tuning could limit ease of deployment. The score reflects a significant contribution with strong potential, but some key challenges remain to be addressed.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Generative Models in Computational Pathology: A Comprehensive Survey on Methods, Applications, and Challenges
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10993v1)
- **Authors**: Yuan Zhang, Xinfeng Zhang, Xiaoming Qi Xinyu Wu, Feng Chen, Guanyu Yang, Huazhu Fu
- **Abstract**: Generative modeling has emerged as a promising direction in computational pathology, offering capabilities such as data-efficient learning, synthetic data augmentation, and multimodal representation across diverse diagnostic tasks. This review provides a comprehensive synthesis of recent progress in the field, organized into four key domains: image generation, text generation, multimodal image-text generation, and other generative applications, including spatial simulation and molecular inference. By analyzing over 150 representative studies, we trace the evolution of generative architectures from early generative adversarial networks to recent advances in diffusion models and foundation models with generative capabilities. We further examine the datasets and evaluation protocols commonly used in this domain and highlight ongoing limitations, including challenges in generating high-fidelity whole slide images, clinical interpretability, and concerns related to the ethical and legal implications of synthetic data. The review concludes with a discussion of open challenges and prospective research directions, with an emphasis on developing unified, multimodal, and clinically deployable generative systems. This work aims to provide a foundational reference for researchers and practitioners developing and applying generative models in computational pathology.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

This paper provides a comprehensive survey of generative models in computational pathology.  It systematically reviews and categorizes research using generative adversarial networks (GANs), variational autoencoders (VAEs), diffusion models, and large language models (LLMs) for tasks like image generation, text generation (reports and captions), and multimodal image-text generation. The paper organizes the surveyed work by generation target, algorithm, application, and dataset. It analyzes over 150 representative studies, highlights common datasets and evaluation metrics, discusses open challenges (like WSI generation fidelity and clinical interpretability), and outlines potential future research directions (focusing on unified, multimodal, and clinically deployable generative systems).  The paper provides a structured taxonomy for this quickly evolving field.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty of the paper lies in its comprehensive, pathology-centric approach to generative models.  While reviews of generative models exist in broader computer vision contexts, this survey specifically targets the domain of computational pathology, which has unique challenges like high-resolution WSIs, stain variations, and clinical report generation. The paper presents a unified taxonomy specifically tailored to pathology applications, addressing a gap in existing literature. The focus on incorporating the advances of Foundation Models and multimodal generation is timely and necessary.
*   **Significance:**  The paper's significance stems from its ability to consolidate a fragmented field and provide a valuable resource for researchers and practitioners.  By systematically organizing the literature, the paper makes it easier for newcomers to understand the key methods, applications, and challenges in this area.  The identification of open challenges and future directions is particularly useful for guiding future research efforts. The organized taxonomy is helpful in understanding the diverse landscape.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensiveness:** Covers a wide range of generative modeling techniques and applications in computational pathology.
    *   **Well-Structured Organization:** The taxonomy by generation target, modeling approach, datasets, and metrics is logical and facilitates understanding.
    *   **Timeliness:**  Addresses the recent surge in interest in generative models, especially with the rise of diffusion models and foundation models.
    *   **Critical Discussion:** Doesn't just list methods but critically discusses the open challenges and limitations of current approaches.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Depth of Technical Detail:** While comprehensive, the technical depth regarding the specific implementation details of each surveyed method is limited.  This is typical of survey papers, but it means the reader might need to consult the original papers for a deeper understanding.
    *   **Limited Focus on Ethical Implications:** While the paper briefly mentions ethical and legal considerations of synthetic data, this area could be expanded. Given the sensitive nature of medical data, a more in-depth discussion of privacy, bias, and fairness would be valuable.
    *   **Rapidly Evolving Field:** Given the fast pace of innovation in generative models, certain aspects of the survey may become dated relatively quickly, necessitating future updates to maintain its relevance.
* **Potential Influence:** The paper has high potential influence. As a central reference for generative models in computational pathology, it could:
    * Help researchers design new models by identifying gaps and limitations in current methods.
    * Facilitate the adoption of these models in clinical settings by addressing challenges such as data availability and computational efficiency.
    * Promote standardization of evaluation metrics and dataset usage within the field.
    * Encourage research in clinically responsible and interpretable model design.
    * Serve as a starting point and overview for those new to the field.

**Justification for Score:**

While a valuable contribution, the survey's reliance on existing literature and the inherent challenge of keeping pace with the field prevent it from reaching the highest level of novelty and impact.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 7/10

### DDAE++: Enhancing Diffusion Models Towards Unified Generative and Discriminative Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10999v1)
- **Authors**: Weilai Xiang, Hongyu Yang, Di Huang, Yunhong Wang
- **Abstract**: While diffusion models have gained prominence in image synthesis, their generative pre-training has been shown to yield discriminative representations, paving the way towards unified visual generation and understanding. However, two key questions remain: 1) Can these representations be leveraged to improve the training of diffusion models themselves, rather than solely benefiting downstream tasks? 2) Can the feature quality be enhanced to rival or even surpass modern self-supervised learners, without compromising generative capability? This work addresses these questions by introducing self-conditioning, a straightforward yet effective mechanism that internally leverages the rich semantics inherent in denoising network to guide its own decoding layers, forming a tighter bottleneck that condenses high-level semantics to improve generation. Results are compelling: our method boosts both generation FID and recognition accuracy with 1% computational overhead and generalizes across diverse diffusion architectures. Crucially, self-conditioning facilitates an effective integration of discriminative techniques, such as contrastive self-distillation, directly into diffusion models without sacrificing generation quality. Extensive experiments on pixel-space and latent-space datasets show that in linear evaluations, our enhanced diffusion models, particularly UViT and DiT, serve as strong representation learners, surpassing various self-supervised models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the DDAE++ paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "DDAE++: Enhancing Diffusion Models Towards Unified Generative and Discriminative Learning" proposes a self-conditioning mechanism to improve both the generative and discriminative capabilities of diffusion models.  The core idea involves leveraging rich semantic features already present within the diffusion model's denoising network and re-injecting these features to guide the decoding process. This aims to create a tighter bottleneck, condense high-level semantics, improve generation quality, and enhance the model's ability to learn discriminative representations.  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their method across diverse diffusion architectures (UNet, UViT, DiT) and integrate contrastive self-distillation for further improvements. Experiments on pixel-space and latent-space datasets show that their enhanced diffusion models, especially UViT and DiT, achieve strong representation learning performance, surpassing various self-supervised methods in linear evaluations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of self-conditioning within diffusion models is moderately novel.  While the original DDAE paper and other studies have explored extracting features from diffusion models, the specific approach of aggregating high-level semantics from within the network and re-injecting them to condition the decoder, coupled with the integration of contrastive self-distillation, distinguishes this work. The application of a [CLS] token within a UViT architecture for this purpose is a novel element, streamlining the process. The study's comprehensive evaluation across different architectures, datasets, and the demonstration of its plug-and-play nature relative to existing diffusion architectures also add novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its effort to bridge the gap between generative and discriminative learning within diffusion models.  Diffusion models are primarily known for their generative power, but their potential for representation learning has been relatively underexplored.  DDAE++ provides a simple and effective method to enhance the discriminative capabilities of diffusion models without sacrificing generative performance.  The fact that the method can be implemented with minimal computational overhead (1% increase) and can even surpass other SSL methods on tasks like linear probing, especially in a class-conditional setting, strengthens the argument for its importance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Simplicity and Generalizability:** The self-conditioning mechanism is simple to implement and generalizes well across different diffusion architectures, making it broadly applicable.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation of its method across diverse datasets, diffusion formulations, and backbone architectures.
    *   **Dual Improvement:** DDAE++ demonstrates simultaneous improvements in both generative (FID) and discriminative (linear probing accuracy) performance.
    *   **Integration of Discriminative Techniques:**  The paper successfully integrates contrastive self-distillation into diffusion models, a pioneering effort in embedding contrastive principles directly within diffusion architectures.
    *   **Strong Results:** The results show that the enhanced diffusion models, particularly UViT and DiT, achieve state-of-the-art representation learning performance.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the method is effective, the improvements in FID are sometimes marginal. In some cases, the performance gains are primarily driven by the discriminative ability while generation is only maintained. While linear probing is a typical evalation metric, it is still a simple measure. More intricate evaluation of feature quality may offer further insight.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Justification:**  The paper lacks a strong theoretical justification for why the proposed self-conditioning mechanism works.  While the authors provide an intuition, a more formal analysis could strengthen the paper.
    *   **Dependence on Augmentation:** The paper notes the reliance on a careful choice of data augmentation strategies, which can be a limitation. Tuning augmentations for different datasets requires more effort.
    *   **Limited Scale:** The evaluations are up to ImageNet1k with 256px images, which is a relatively moderate scale by today's standards. The claim needs to be validated on even larger scale datasets and higher resolution images to further cement its importance.
    *   **Ablation is limited to simpler models**: While the authors include ablation studies, it would be good to run it on UViT models to ensure effectiveness there.

*   **Potential Influence:**  DDAE++ has the potential to influence future research on diffusion models by:

    *   Encouraging further exploration of the discriminative capabilities of diffusion models.
    *   Providing a simple and effective method to improve the representation learning performance of diffusion models.
    *   Facilitating the integration of other discriminative techniques into diffusion models.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

DDAE++ presents a useful contribution to the field by making diffusion models more versatile foundation models. The work is well-executed, with a clear and simple mechanism, and is demonstrated across a strong set of evaluations. While the individual contributions are not revolutionary, the combination of self-conditioning, diverse architectures and integration of contrastive learning makes the work reasonably novel. Some theoretical grounding would increase the work's impact. Stronger results and ablation over the UViT models would definitely push this towards higher scores. The limitations in scale also prevent it from being more influential. However, the simplicity and generalizability make it a valuable addition to the community. The score reflects the solid contribution, offset by the incremental improvements and relatively underexplored limitations that should be addressed in the future.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Review-Instruct: A Review-Driven Multi-Turn Conversations Generation Method for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11010v1)
- **Authors**: Jiangxu Wu, Cong Wang, TianHuang Su, Jun Yang, Haozhi Lin, Chao Zhang, Ming Peng, Kai Shi, SongPan Yang, BinQing Pan, ZiXian Li, Ni Yang, ZhenYu Yang
- **Abstract**: The effectiveness of large language models (LLMs) in conversational AI is hindered by their reliance on single-turn supervised fine-tuning (SFT) data, which limits contextual coherence in multi-turn dialogues. Existing methods for generating multi-turn dialogue data struggle to ensure both diversity and quality in instructions. To address this, we propose Review-Instruct, a novel framework that synthesizes multi-turn conversations through an iterative "Ask-Respond-Review" process involving three agent roles: a Candidate, multiple Reviewers, and a Chairman. The framework iteratively refines instructions by incorporating Reviewer feedback, enhancing dialogue diversity and difficulty. We construct a multi-turn dataset using the Alpaca dataset and fine-tune the LLaMA2-13B model. Evaluations on MT-Bench, MMLU-Pro, and Auto-Arena demonstrate significant improvements, achieving absolute gains of 2.9\% on MMLU-Pro and 2\% on MT-Bench compared to prior state-of-the-art models based on LLaMA2-13B. Ablation studies confirm the critical role of the Review stage and the use of multiple Reviewers in boosting instruction diversity and difficulty. Our work highlights the potential of review-driven, multi-agent frameworks for generating high-quality conversational data at scale.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "Review-Instruct: A Review-Driven Multi-Turn Conversations Generation Method for Large Language Models" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Review-Instruct, a novel framework for generating high-quality, multi-turn dialogue data for fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs).  It addresses the limitations of relying solely on single-turn data for training, which hinders contextual coherence in longer conversations. Review-Instruct uses an iterative "Ask-Respond-Review" process involving three AI agents: a Candidate (generates responses), multiple Reviewers (evaluate responses), and a Chairman (synthesizes follow-up instructions). The framework leverages feedback from reviewers to iteratively refine instructions, enhancing dialogue diversity and complexity.  Experiments show significant improvements on benchmarks like MT-Bench, MMLU-Pro, and Auto-Arena compared to existing LLaMA2-13B based models. Ablation studies confirm the importance of the Review stage and the benefits of using multiple reviewers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a clear and novel approach to multi-turn dialogue generation.  The core idea of incorporating a Review stage, inspired by Auto-Arena's evaluation methodologies, into the Ask-Respond paradigm is a significant departure from existing dual-agent methods.  The framework's three-agent architecture and iterative refinement loop represent a valuable contribution. **However, the core concept of iterative refinement based on feedback is not entirely new, and elements of it can be seen in other data augmentation or self-improvement techniques used in language modeling. Therefore, while the specific implementation is novel, the underlying principle has some precedent.**

*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates strong empirical results.  The reported gains on MT-Bench, MMLU-Pro, and Auto-Arena are compelling and highlight the effectiveness of the Review-Instruct framework.  The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the importance of the Review stage and the benefits of multiple reviewers. The analysis of instruction difficulty and diversity strengthens the paper's claims and offers a deeper understanding of the framework's impact. **The ability to generate more challenging and diverse conversational data addresses a crucial bottleneck in training effective conversational AI systems.**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Framework:** The Review-Instruct framework is well-defined and easy to understand.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents convincing evidence of the framework's effectiveness.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The ablation studies and dataset analysis provide valuable insights.
    *   **Practical Application:** The framework leverages existing instruction datasets and can be integrated with other data generation methods.
    *   **Multi-agent Approach:** The use of multiple Reviewers helps to ensure diversity in the evaluations and the synthesis of more comprehensive instructions.
    *   **Addresses a Key Challenge:** The paper tackles a critical problem in conversational AI - generating high-quality, multi-turn dialogue data.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on Base LLMs:** The framework's effectiveness depends on the capabilities of the underlying LLMs used for the Candidate, Reviewer, and Chairman roles. The quality of the generated dialogues is limited by the knowledge and reasoning abilities of these LLMs.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The Review stage adds computational overhead compared to simpler Ask-Respond methods.
    *   **Bias Potential:** The selection of LLMs for the Reviewer roles could introduce biases into the generated dialogues.
    *   **Generalizability Limitations:** Experiments are primarily based on the Alpaca dataset. The extent to which Review-Instruct generalizes to other instruction datasets or conversational domains is unclear.
    *   **Limited Multi-turn Depth:** While the method generates multi-turn dialogues, the experiments were limited to only 2 turns. The long-term coherence and consistency of conversations generated using Review-Instruct should be further evaluated.
    *   **Limited Baseline Comparison:** A more comprehensive comparison with more recent multi-turn dialogue generation techniques would strengthen the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** The Review-Instruct framework has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical and effective method for generating high-quality, multi-turn dialogue data. It could be used to improve the performance of conversational AI systems across a range of tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to multi-turn dialogue generation. The empirical results are compelling, and the analysis provides valuable insights. However, the reliance on base LLMs, the potential for bias, and the limited generalizability are significant weaknesses. Furthermore, while the implementation is novel, the underlying principle of iterative refinement has precedent.
Therefore, a score of **7** is appropriate. It reflects a significant contribution to the field, but one that has limitations and room for improvement. The paper's impact will depend on how well Review-Instruct generalizes to other datasets and how effectively it can be scaled to generate longer and more complex conversations.

### Humans expect rationality and cooperation from LLM opponents in strategic games
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11011v1)
- **Authors**: Darija Barak, Miguel Costa-Gomes
- **Abstract**: As Large Language Models (LLMs) integrate into our social and economic interactions, we need to deepen our understanding of how humans respond to LLMs opponents in strategic settings. We present the results of the first controlled monetarily-incentivised laboratory experiment looking at differences in human behaviour in a multi-player p-beauty contest against other humans and LLMs. We use a within-subject design in order to compare behaviour at the individual level. We show that, in this environment, human subjects choose significantly lower numbers when playing against LLMs than humans, which is mainly driven by the increased prevalence of `zero' Nash-equilibrium choices. This shift is mainly driven by subjects with high strategic reasoning ability. Subjects who play the zero Nash-equilibrium choice motivate their strategy by appealing to perceived LLM's reasoning ability and, unexpectedly, propensity towards cooperation. Our findings provide foundational insights into the multi-player human-LLM interaction in simultaneous choice games, uncover heterogeneities in both subjects' behaviour and beliefs about LLM's play when playing against them, and suggest important implications for mechanism design in mixed human-LLM systems.
- **Summary**: Here is a concise summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper.

**Summary:**

This paper presents the results of a laboratory experiment investigating how humans behave in strategic environments when interacting with multiple Large Language Model (LLM) opponents, specifically focusing on the p-beauty contest game. The researchers use a within-subject design to compare human choices when playing against other humans versus against LLMs (ChatGPT 3.5 and Claude v2). Key findings include: humans choose significantly lower numbers when playing against LLMs, driven by an increased prevalence of 'zero' choices; this effect is more pronounced among subjects with high strategic reasoning ability; and subjects who choose zero often justify their strategy by appealing to perceived LLM reasoning ability and propensity towards cooperation. The study provides initial insights into human-LLM strategic interactions, highlighting behavioral heterogeneities and beliefs about LLM behavior.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper represents a novel and valuable contribution as it directly explores how humans react *strategically* to LLMs. Previous works have examined LLM behavior itself or focused on simpler human-LLM interactions (e.g., prisoner's dilemma). This work's key contribution lies in addressing the gap in understanding multi-player strategic environments where no dominant strategy exists and humans need to form beliefs about LLM opponents' actions.  The use of a within-subject design, allowing for individual-level comparison, strengthens the study.

**Significance:** The findings offer important implications for mechanism design in future economic systems increasingly incorporating AI agents. Understanding how humans perceive and strategically interact with LLMs is crucial for designing effective mechanisms in markets, negotiations, and other economic activities. The paper's emphasis on behavioral heterogeneities (e.g., the difference in responses based on strategic reasoning ability) is a significant contribution that many other studies might gloss over.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Research Question:** Addresses an important and timely issue regarding human-AI strategic interaction.
*   **Rigorous Methodology:** Uses a well-controlled laboratory experiment with monetary incentives.  The within-subject design is a powerful tool for comparative analysis.
*   **Interesting Findings:** The observed shift towards Nash equilibrium choices when facing LLMs, particularly among strategically sophisticated subjects, and the stated justifications (reasoning ability and cooperation) are compelling.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and organized, clearly outlining the experimental design, results, and implications.
*   **Careful data analysis:** The paper carefully considers factors like the ordering of the games.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The study focuses on a single game (p-beauty contest) and specific LLMs. The generalizability to other strategic environments and newer LLM architectures remains to be seen. The study only includes LLMs as opponents, without considering LLMs as potential teammates, which could also provide relevant insights.
*   **Small Sample Size:** While adequate for a lab experiment, the sample size (n=72) may limit the power to detect subtle effects. A larger and more diverse sample would strengthen the external validity of the findings. The fact that the student population is relatively homogeneous raises questions of external validity.
*   **Potential Confounding Factors:**  Although the authors address the issue of introspective learning in the ordering of tasks, other potential confounding factors (e.g. how the particular LLM "personalities" influence humans) might still exist. Also the authors only looked at 2 LLM prompts (ChatGPT 3.5, Claude v2), but different versions of the prompt could dramatically affect the interaction.
*   **Qualitative Data Limited:** The qualitative data on motivations, while insightful, relies on a relatively small subset of subjects who made zero choices. A deeper qualitative analysis across a broader range of subjects would enrich the findings.
*   **No analysis of cognitive effort involved:** The participants play against both humans and LLMs, but the authors do not control for any cognitive fatigue effects. They also do not examine the differences in thinking time between when the participants play against humans and LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable initial contribution to an underexplored area of research. It is innovative, uses a strong methodological approach, and identifies interesting behavioral patterns with significant implications.  However, limitations in scope, sample size, and potential confounding factors prevent it from being considered a truly exceptional contribution. Also some of the findings (e.g. introspective learning) are not novel, as the same introspective learning patterns have been found in multiple similar experimental studies.

### WildDoc: How Far Are We from Achieving Comprehensive and Robust Document Understanding in the Wild?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11015v1)
- **Authors**: An-Lan Wang, Jingqun Tang, Liao Lei, Hao Feng, Qi Liu, Xiang Fei, Jinghui Lu, Han Wang, Weiwei Liu, Hao Liu, Yuliang Liu, Xiang Bai, Can Huang
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancements in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have significantly enhanced capabilities in Document Understanding. However, prevailing benchmarks like DocVQA and ChartQA predominantly comprise \textit{scanned or digital} documents, inadequately reflecting the intricate challenges posed by diverse real-world scenarios, such as variable illumination and physical distortions. This paper introduces WildDoc, the inaugural benchmark designed specifically for assessing document understanding in natural environments. WildDoc incorporates a diverse set of manually captured document images reflecting real-world conditions and leverages document sources from established benchmarks to facilitate comprehensive comparisons with digital or scanned documents. Further, to rigorously evaluate model robustness, each document is captured four times under different conditions. Evaluations of state-of-the-art MLLMs on WildDoc expose substantial performance declines and underscore the models' inadequate robustness compared to traditional benchmarks, highlighting the unique challenges posed by real-world document understanding. Our project homepage is available at https://bytedance.github.io/WildDoc.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces WildDoc, a new benchmark dataset designed to evaluate the robustness and comprehensive document understanding capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) in real-world scenarios.  Unlike existing benchmarks that primarily use scanned or digital documents, WildDoc consists of manually captured document images under varying environmental conditions (illumination, view, distortion, effects). The benchmark uses document sources from existing datasets to enable comparisons between performance on clean (scanned/digital) vs. real-world images.  The authors also introduce a "consistency score" metric to assess model robustness across different image capture conditions of the same document. Experiments with several state-of-the-art MLLMs demonstrate a significant performance drop on WildDoc compared to traditional benchmarks, highlighting the limited robustness of current models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has moderate novelty. The core idea of creating a real-world document understanding benchmark is valuable and addresses a clear gap in the field. While the concept of evaluating models in more realistic conditions isn't entirely new (other domains have explored robustness), its application to document understanding is a meaningful contribution. The "consistency score" metric, while simple, is a reasonable approach to quantify model robustness across different conditions.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper lies in exposing the limitations of current MLLMs in real-world scenarios. It highlights that performance on existing benchmarks may not accurately reflect a model's true capabilities when deployed in less controlled environments. This is crucial for guiding future research towards developing more robust and practical document understanding systems.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly identifies a limitation of existing benchmarks and proposes a solution.
    *   The WildDoc dataset is well-motivated and designed to capture important real-world variations.
    *   The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the performance gap between traditional and real-world document understanding.
    *   The consistency metric provides a tangible way to evaluate robustness.
    *   The paper is well-written and organized.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the paper mentions different environmental conditions, the specific selection of those conditions and their variations might be somewhat arbitrary. A more systematic analysis of the most impactful real-world challenges could be beneficial.
    *   The reliance on existing datasets as document sources, while practical, might limit the scope of document types and layouts represented in WildDoc.  It introduces a potential bias by only considering those already present.
    *   The consistency metric, while helpful, is a relatively simple measure of robustness. More sophisticated metrics that consider the severity of the variations or the types of errors made could be explored.
    *   The paper does not address the challenge of evaluating models with differing prompting strategies beyond the standard approach inherited from source datasets, which may significantly affect overall model performance and fairness in evaluation.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a solid contribution to the field of document understanding. It identifies and addresses a real limitation in existing benchmarks, leading to actionable insights for future research. While the technical novelty might be considered moderate, the practical significance of highlighting the gap between lab performance and real-world performance justifies a high score. The strengths outweigh the weaknesses, and the WildDoc benchmark has the potential to influence the direction of research in this area.

### Logo-LLM: Local and Global Modeling with Large Language Models for Time Series Forecasting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11017v1)
- **Authors**: Wenjie Ou, Zhishuo Zhao, Dongyue Guo, Yi Lin
- **Abstract**: Time series forecasting is critical across multiple domains, where time series data exhibits both local patterns and global dependencies. While Transformer-based methods effectively capture global dependencies, they often overlook short-term local variations in time series. Recent methods that adapt large language models (LLMs) into time series forecasting inherit this limitation by treating LLMs as black-box encoders, relying solely on the final-layer output and underutilizing hierarchical representations. To address this limitation, we propose Logo-LLM, a novel LLM-based framework that explicitly extracts and models multi-scale temporal features from different layers of a pre-trained LLM. Through empirical analysis, we show that shallow layers of LLMs capture local dynamics in time series, while deeper layers encode global trends. Moreover, Logo-LLM introduces lightweight Local-Mixer and Global-Mixer modules to align and integrate features with the temporal input across layers. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Logo-LLM achieves superior performance across diverse benchmarks, with strong generalization in few-shot and zero-shot settings while maintaining low computational overhead.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Logo-LLM: Local and Global Modeling with Large Language Models for Time Series Forecasting":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Logo-LLM, a novel framework for time series forecasting that leverages pre-trained Large Language Models (LLMs) by explicitly extracting and modeling multi-scale temporal features from different layers. It argues that shallow layers of LLMs capture local dynamics, while deeper layers encode global trends. Logo-LLM employs Local-Mixer and Global-Mixer modules to align and integrate features from different layers with the temporal input. The framework achieves state-of-the-art or comparable performance across diverse benchmarks, demonstrating strong generalization in few-shot and zero-shot settings while maintaining low computational overhead.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The key novelty lies in the hierarchical feature extraction from different layers of LLMs for time series forecasting.  Prior work typically treated LLMs as black boxes, using only the final layer output. The paper presents empirical evidence to support the claim that different LLM layers capture distinct temporal dynamics, which motivates the design of Logo-LLM to explicitly exploit this hierarchical representation. The introduction of Local-Mixer and Global-Mixer modules to align the extracted features is also a novel contribution. It should be noted that the vision transformer literature has observed similar layer-wise feature differences. The novelty also stems from the application of these ideas to a time series context in conjunction with LLMs, and the subsequent modules designed to best leverage these features.

**Significance:**

The paper has significant implications for time series forecasting with LLMs.  By demonstrating that LLMs can effectively model both local and global temporal dependencies through hierarchical feature extraction, it opens up new avenues for research.  The strong performance in few-shot and zero-shot settings is particularly valuable, as it suggests that LLMs can generalize to new time series data with limited or no task-specific training. The reported low computational overhead makes the approach practical for real-world applications. The comprehensive ablation studies justify the design choices and prove the effectiveness of the design.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Motivation:** The paper clearly identifies a limitation of existing LLM-based time series forecasting methods.
*   **Empirical Evidence:** The paper provides strong empirical evidence to support its claims about layer-wise feature specialization in LLMs.
*   **Novel Architecture:** The Logo-LLM framework is well-designed and incorporates novel components like Local-Mixer and Global-Mixer.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:**  The paper presents extensive experimental results across diverse benchmarks and settings (long-term, few-shot, zero-shot).
*   **Ablation Studies:** Rigorous ablation studies are performed that validate the design.
*   **Computational Efficiency:** The paper addresses the computational efficiency concerns with experiments about model size and complexity.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Theoretical Analysis:** The paper lacks a rigorous theoretical analysis of the layer-wise feature specialization in LLMs.  While the empirical evidence is compelling, a more formal understanding of why this phenomenon occurs would strengthen the paper.
*   **Limited Evaluation of World Knowledge:** While the introduction highlights the LLMs leveraging extensive world knowledge, it does not investigate how the world knowledge affect the model in detail.
*   **Limited novel loss or training techniques:** The paper does not include any advanced training methods, or a specific design for loss functions to further promote more effective representations.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field of time series forecasting with LLMs by proposing a novel and effective framework for hierarchical feature extraction. The empirical results are convincing, and the ablation studies demonstrate the importance of the key design choices. While the lack of a more rigorous theoretical analysis is a minor weakness, the paper's overall impact on the field is significant. It is worth noting that the novelty may be slightly reduced due to similar findings in the vision transformer domain, even though the application and context here is distinct.

### OntoURL: A Benchmark for Evaluating Large Language Models on Symbolic Ontological Understanding, Reasoning and Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11031v1)
- **Authors**: Xiao Zhang, Huiyuan Lai, Qianru Meng, Johan Bos
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities across a range of natural language processing tasks, yet their ability to process structured symbolic knowledge remains underexplored. To address this gap, we propose a taxonomy of LLMs' ontological capabilities and introduce OntoURL, the first comprehensive benchmark designed to systematically evaluate LLMs' proficiency in handling ontologies -- formal, symbolic representations of domain knowledge through concepts, relationships, and instances. Based on the proposed taxonomy, OntoURL systematically assesses three dimensions: understanding, reasoning, and learning through 15 distinct tasks comprising 58,981 questions derived from 40 ontologies across 8 domains. Experiments with 20 open-source LLMs reveal significant performance differences across models, tasks, and domains, with current LLMs showing proficiency in understanding ontological knowledge but substantial weaknesses in reasoning and learning tasks. These findings highlight fundamental limitations in LLMs' capability to process symbolic knowledge and establish OntoURL as a critical benchmark for advancing the integration of LLMs with formal knowledge representations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "OntoURL: A Benchmark for Evaluating Large Language Models on Symbolic Ontological Understanding, Reasoning and Learning" introduces a new benchmark, ONTOURL, designed to systematically assess the capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) in handling ontologies.  Ontologies are formal, symbolic representations of domain knowledge, consisting of concepts, relationships, and instances.  The authors argue that while LLMs have demonstrated impressive performance on many NLP tasks, their ability to process structured symbolic knowledge, as represented by ontologies, remains largely unexplored.

ONTOURL evaluates LLMs across three dimensions: understanding, reasoning, and learning. It comprises 58,981 questions derived from 40 ontologies across 8 domains. The authors propose a taxonomy of LLM ontological capabilities based on Bloom's Taxonomy to guide the creation of benchmark tasks. The paper presents experimental results from evaluating 20 open-source LLMs, highlighting significant performance differences across models, tasks, and domains. Results show that LLMs demonstrate competency in ontological understanding but struggle with reasoning and learning tasks. The authors conclude that fundamental limitations exist in LLMs' ability to process symbolic knowledge and that ONTOURL serves as a vital benchmark for advancing LLM integration with formal knowledge representations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The creation of a comprehensive benchmark focused specifically on evaluating LLMs' ability to handle ontologies is a valuable contribution. Previous datasets have often focused on isolated aspects of ontologies or were not specifically designed for LLMs. The taxonomy of ontological capabilities is a useful framework. The comprehensive design, with three axes of evaluation (understanding, reasoning, learning) is a strong point.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial gap in research: evaluating LLMs' ability to interact with structured symbolic knowledge. Ontologies are fundamental to many knowledge-intensive applications (e.g., biomedicine, finance, legal domains). If LLMs can effectively leverage ontologies, it could lead to significant advancements in these fields. The findings highlight current LLMs' limitations, thereby setting a clear research direction. The scale of the benchmark (number of questions, ontologies, and models evaluated) contributes to the paper's impact.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The ONTOURL benchmark offers a multifaceted assessment of LLMs across understanding, reasoning, and learning abilities within ontological contexts. The extensive dataset is a major plus.
    *   **Clear Taxonomy:** The proposed taxonomy for classifying LLM ontological capabilities provides a systematic framework for analyzing LLMs' interactions with structured symbolic knowledge.
    *   **Thorough Evaluation of Models:** Comprehensive experiments involving multiple LLMs illuminate strengths and weaknesses of current models.
    *   **Addresses a Relevant and Underexplored Area:** The focus on symbolic reasoning with ontologies is timely, given the broader discussions around grounding and symbolic AI.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limitations of Automatic Metrics:** The reliance on automatic metrics for evaluating the generative tasks (L1-L5) is a potential weakness. While ROUGE-L and F1 scores provide an indication of performance, they may not fully capture the semantic correctness or logical consistency of the generated outputs.  Human evaluation is mentioned as a future direction, and its absence is noted.
    *   **Potential for Benchmark Bias:** While the authors use a diverse set of ontologies, there is a risk of bias towards certain domains or types of ontologies. The sciences domain is heavily represented.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Advanced Techniques:** While the evaluation provides insights into LLMs' baseline capabilities, it could benefit from exploring more advanced prompting techniques or fine-tuning strategies.

*   **Potential Influence:**  ONTOURL has the potential to become a standard benchmark for the community, similar to GLUE or SuperGLUE for general NLP.  It can stimulate research into improving LLMs' symbolic reasoning abilities and inform the design of new models and techniques for ontology-related tasks.

*Rigorous rationale for the score assigned:*

The paper introduces a significant, comprehensive, and timely benchmark, but its weaknesses prevent it from being considered an exceptional contribution at this point. The limitations regarding the reliance on automatic metrics for generation tasks and the limited exploration of advanced techniques are not inconsequential. Nevertheless, the scope, rigor, and relevance of the benchmark warrant a rating that acknowledges the value of the work, but also the space for significant future improvements that are already outlined in the paper.
