### MPMA: Preference Manipulation Attack Against Model Context Protocol
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11154v1)
- **Authors**: Zihan Wang, Hongwei Li, Rui Zhang, Yu Liu, Wenbo Jiang, Wenshu Fan, Qingchuan Zhao, Guowen Xu
- **Abstract**: Model Context Protocol (MCP) standardizes interface mapping for large language models (LLMs) to access external data and tools, which revolutionizes the paradigm of tool selection and facilitates the rapid expansion of the LLM agent tool ecosystem. However, as the MCP is increasingly adopted, third-party customized versions of the MCP server expose potential security vulnerabilities. In this paper, we first introduce a novel security threat, which we term the MCP Preference Manipulation Attack (MPMA). An attacker deploys a customized MCP server to manipulate LLMs, causing them to prioritize it over other competing MCP servers. This can result in economic benefits for attackers, such as revenue from paid MCP services or advertising income generated from free servers. To achieve MPMA, we first design a Direct Preference Manipulation Attack ($\mathtt{DPMA}$) that achieves significant effectiveness by inserting the manipulative word and phrases into the tool name and description. However, such a direct modification is obvious to users and lacks stealthiness. To address these limitations, we further propose Genetic-based Advertising Preference Manipulation Attack ($\mathtt{GAPMA}$). $\mathtt{GAPMA}$ employs four commonly used strategies to initialize descriptions and integrates a Genetic Algorithm (GA) to enhance stealthiness. The experiment results demonstrate that $\mathtt{GAPMA}$ balances high effectiveness and stealthiness. Our study reveals a critical vulnerability of the MCP in open ecosystems, highlighting an urgent need for robust defense mechanisms to ensure the fairness of the MCP ecosystem.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MPMA: Preference Manipulation Attack Against Model Context Protocol":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel security threat called the MCP Preference Manipulation Attack (MPMA) targeting the Model Context Protocol (MCP). MPMA involves an attacker deploying a malicious MCP server to manipulate LLMs, causing them to prioritize the malicious server over others. The paper proposes two attack strategies: (1) Directly Preference Manipulation Attack (DPMA), which inserts manipulative words directly into the tool name and description, and (2) Genetic-based Advertising Preference Manipulation Attack (GAPMA), which employs advertising strategies and a genetic algorithm to enhance stealthiness. The paper evaluates these strategies across several MCP servers and LLMs, demonstrating the vulnerability of the MCP-based tool selection process to MPMA and highlighting the need for defense mechanisms. The results show that while DPMA achieves high attack success rates, GAPMA balances effectiveness and stealthiness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a new security threat, MPMA, specific to the emerging MCP framework. This is a timely contribution as the MCP ecosystem is rapidly growing and security vulnerabilities need to be addressed proactively. The novelty lies in specifically targeting the tool selection process within MCP using preference manipulation. The use of genetic algorithms and advertising strategies to enhance stealthiness is also a novel approach in this context.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings are significant because they expose a vulnerability in the MCP framework that could be exploited for economic gain by malicious actors. By demonstrating how an attacker can manipulate LLMs into preferring their MCP server, the paper highlights the potential for unfair competition and disruption within the MCP ecosystem. The paper's analysis of stealthiness and the development of GAPMA address a crucial aspect of real-world attack scenarios. The economic loss estimations, while rough, further emphasize the importance of the threat.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clearly defines the problem and the attack scenario.
    *   Presents two well-defined attack strategies, DPMA and GAPMA.
    *   Thorough experimental evaluation across multiple LLMs and MCP servers.
    *   Addresses the critical aspect of stealthiness in the attacks.
    *   Provides estimations of potential economic impact.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The economic loss estimations, while useful for context, are based on several assumptions and may not be entirely accurate. More detailed economic modelling may benefit future work.
    *   The evaluation, while comprehensive, could benefit from examining real-world responses or user feedback. The LLM-as-a-judge has its inherent weaknesses.
    *   The limitations section acknowledges the dependence on the LLM's internal selection mechanism but provides only a hypothesis as to why certain LLMs perform differently.
    *   The study primarily focuses on the attack side; detailed defensive mechanisms are not proposed but left for future work.

*   **Justification of the Score:**

The paper is a significant contribution to the field because it identifies a new vulnerability in a rapidly evolving technology (MCP). The strategies introduced (DPMA and GAPMA) are well-defined and the experimental evaluation is thorough and contributes novel insights. The paper also acknowledges and explores the critical dimension of stealth and how to achieve it in such attacks.

However, there are some limitations. The study does not delve into proposing and evaluating specific defense measures to counter the attack. While the economic impact is estimated, these estimations are not rigorously substantiated and rely on a number of assumptions.

Considering these strengths and limitations, the paper presents valuable insights and demonstrates new vulnerabilities to an important emerging framework. It is not a groundbreaking paper with completely novel scientific findings, but it performs a needed and detailed assessment of the vulnerability landscape to this emerging framework, the Model Context Protocol.

### Attention on the Sphere
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11157v1)
- **Authors**: Boris Bonev, Max Rietmann, Andrea Paris, Alberto Carpentieri, Thorsten Kurth
- **Abstract**: We introduce a generalized attention mechanism for spherical domains, enabling Transformer architectures to natively process data defined on the two-dimensional sphere - a critical need in fields such as atmospheric physics, cosmology, and robotics, where preserving spherical symmetries and topology is essential for physical accuracy. By integrating numerical quadrature weights into the attention mechanism, we obtain a geometrically faithful spherical attention that is approximately rotationally equivariant, providing strong inductive biases and leading to better performance than Cartesian approaches. To further enhance both scalability and model performance, we propose neighborhood attention on the sphere, which confines interactions to geodesic neighborhoods. This approach reduces computational complexity and introduces the additional inductive bias for locality, while retaining the symmetry properties of our method. We provide optimized CUDA kernels and memory-efficient implementations to ensure practical applicability. The method is validated on three diverse tasks: simulating shallow water equations on the rotating sphere, spherical image segmentation, and spherical depth estimation. Across all tasks, our spherical Transformers consistently outperform their planar counterparts, highlighting the advantage of geometric priors for learning on spherical domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel generalized attention mechanism specifically designed for spherical domains. This enables the direct processing of data on the 2D sphere using Transformer architectures. The key idea is to incorporate numerical quadrature weights into the attention mechanism, resulting in a geometrically faithful spherical attention that approximates rotational equivariance. This approach provides strong inductive biases, leading to improved performance compared to Cartesian-based methods. The authors also propose a "neighborhood attention" variant for the sphere, which restricts interactions to geodesic neighborhoods, reducing computational complexity and introducing locality while maintaining symmetry properties. The method is validated on tasks like shallow water equation simulation, spherical image segmentation, and depth estimation, demonstrating that spherical Transformers outperform planar counterparts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in its geometric interpretation of attention on the sphere. While prior works have applied Transformers to spherical data, this paper offers a continuous formulation of attention with quadrature integration, offering a more geometrically faithful implementation compared to graph based and other discrete approaches. The neighborhood attention variant is also a worthwhile contribution. It leverages geodesic distance for computational efficiency and local reasoning on the sphere, preserving equivariance properties. The application of discrete-continuous convolution for the encoder-decoder architecture is also noteworthy.

* **Significance:** The significance is high, particularly in scientific domains like atmospheric physics, cosmology, and robotics, where spherical data is prevalent, and preserving symmetries is crucial. The ability to directly process spherical data with Transformers, capturing long-range dependencies while respecting geometric properties, addresses a significant gap. The CUDA-optimized implementation and validation on diverse tasks (PDE solving, segmentation, depth estimation) increase the practical impact. While single step prediction performance is remarkable, none of the architectures tested show stability in autoregressive rollouts which is important for physical models.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Geometric Fidelity:** The main strength is the careful integration of spherical geometry through quadrature rules. This addresses a limitation of prior methods relying on planar approximations or discrete graph structures.
    * **Equivariance:** The method maintains approximate rotational equivariance, which is essential for physical accuracy in many applications.
    * **Scalability:** The neighborhood attention mechanism improves scalability, enabling application to higher-resolution spherical data.
    * **Practical Implementation:** The authors provide optimized CUDA kernels and memory-efficient implementations, making the method usable in practice.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation covers diverse tasks and compares against relevant baselines, highlighting the benefits of the spherical approach.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Computational cost:** The custom CUDA implementations for spherical neighborhood attention are slower than optimized SDPA kernels.
    * **Limited autoregressive experiments:** Stability in autoregressive rollouts is not demostrated and therefore the models might not have the necessary accuracy for weather or climate modelling, and should be further studied.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has a good potential to influence research on deep learning for spherical data and scientific computing, leading to further developments in geometric deep learning and more accurate models for spherical domains. The public code release and thorough evaluation contribute to its usability and adoption.

* **Rigorous Rationale:** The incorporation of geometrical information in the architecture is innovative and contributes in a positive way to performance, while allowing to properly encode the inherent symmetries of the spherical domain, but not without cost. There is room for improvement in computational efficiency as well as improvement in long-term autoregressive forecasting.

**Score: 7.5**

This score reflects the paper's novelty in geometrically informed attention for spherical domains, its practical implementation and diverse validation, and the potential for impact in scientific applications. However, the current computational cost and not yet fully satisfactory long-term forecasting capabilities prevent a higher score. Future work could address these limitations, further increasing its influence.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Diffusion Model in Hyperspectral Image Processing and Analysis: A Review
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11158v1)
- **Authors**: Xing Hu, Xiangcheng Liu, Qianqian Duan, Danfeng Hong, Dawei Zhang
- **Abstract**: Hyperspectral image processing and analysis has important application value in remote sensing, agriculture and environmental monitoring, but its high dimensionality, data redundancy and noise interference etc. bring great challenges to the analysis. Traditional models have limitations in dealing with these complex data, and it is difficult to meet the increasing demand for analysis. In recent years, Diffusion Model, as an emerging generative model, has shown unique advantages in hyperspectral image processing. By simulating the diffusion process of data in time, the Diffusion Model can effectively process high-dimensional data, generate high-quality samples, and perform well in denoising and data enhancement. In this paper, we review the recent research advances in diffusion modeling for hyperspectral image processing and analysis, and discuss its applications in tasks such as high-dimensional data processing, noise removal, classification, and anomaly detection. The performance of diffusion-based models on image processing is compared and the challenges are summarized. It is shown that the diffusion model can significantly improve the accuracy and efficiency of hyperspectral image analysis, providing a new direction for future research.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper.

**Summary**

This paper presents a review of the application of diffusion models (DMs) in hyperspectral image (HSI) processing and analysis. It begins by outlining the challenges associated with HSIs, such as high dimensionality, data redundancy, and noise interference, that traditional methods struggle to overcome. The paper then introduces diffusion models as an emerging generative approach, highlighting their potential advantages in handling high-dimensional data, generating high-quality samples, and performing denoising and data enhancement.

The review categorizes the existing diffusion models into three groups: Denoising Diffusion Probabilistic Models (DDPM), Score-Based Generative Models (SGM), and Stochastic Differential Equation-based models (SDE). The paper discusses the theoretical foundations of these models, their evolution with different variants, and compares them with traditional deep learning models (GANs, VAEs, Autoregressive Models). It further explores the diverse applications of diffusion models in HSI processing, including data enhancement and synthesis, data generation and complementation, classification, segmentation, target detection, anomaly detection, noise suppression, and data recovery. Finally, the paper summarizes the challenges and limitations of diffusion models in HSI processing, such as computational complexity, dependence on large labeled datasets, adaptability to large-scale data, and interpretability issues. The paper concludes by providing an outlook on future research directions, focusing on fusion innovation, optimization and deployment, and automated model design. Experimental results are provided to demonstrate improvements in performance.

**Critical Evaluation**

**Novelty:** While the paper provides a comprehensive overview of diffusion models in HSI processing, its primary contribution lies in the systematic organization and consolidation of existing knowledge. It does not introduce any new models, algorithms, or theoretical frameworks. However, its novelty resides in:

*   **Comprehensive Taxonomy:** The paper offers a structured classification of diffusion models and their applications within the specific context of HSI analysis, providing a useful framework for understanding the field.
*   **Emphasis on Practical Challenges:** The paper delves into the specific limitations and challenges of applying diffusion models to HSIs, highlighting issues such as computational complexity and the need for large labeled datasets, which are often overlooked in more general reviews.
*   **Detailed Review of Recent Advances:** Includes more recent diffusion-based work, and provide a more comprehensive review on the theoretical development of diffusion model.

**Significance:** The paper makes a significant contribution by synthesizing the scattered research efforts in the field and providing a coherent overview of the current state of the art. This is valuable for researchers and practitioners seeking to understand the potential of diffusion models for HSI processing. The paper also highlights the limitations and challenges that need to be addressed for practical deployment, which can guide future research directions.

**Strengths:**

*   Well-organized and comprehensive review
*   Clear explanation of complex theoretical concepts
*   Balanced assessment of the advantages and limitations of diffusion models
*   Identification of promising research directions

**Weaknesses:**

*   Lacks in-depth analysis of specific HSI properties and their interaction with diffusion models
*   The scope of research is wide but it is a summarization of diffusion model-based papers, and less critical investigation to explore the specific contribution.
*   Limited experimental verification of the diffusion model methods and practical engineering constraint analysis.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** to this paper.

**Rationale:**

*   A score of 7 indicates that the paper makes a solid contribution to the field through its comprehensive review and critical assessment of diffusion models for HSI processing. The work provides a useful overview and highlights important challenges and limitations.

    **However:** It stops short of introducing original methodological insights or providing substantial validation through experimentation, limiting its overall impact. It is essentially a survey or review paper that can serve as a foundation for further research.
*   The paper does a great job of summarizing the current progress and direction in diffusion model in hyperspectral image processing, it can clearly provide a guidance on the readers.

- **Score**: 7/10

### SoLoPO: Unlocking Long-Context Capabilities in LLMs via Short-to-Long Preference Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11166v1)
- **Authors**: Huashan Sun, Shengyi Liao, Yansen Han, Yu Bai, Yang Gao, Cheng Fu, Weizhou Shen, Fanqi Wan, Ming Yan, Ji Zhang, Fei Huang
- **Abstract**: Despite advances in pretraining with extended context lengths, large language models (LLMs) still face challenges in effectively utilizing real-world long-context information, primarily due to insufficient long-context alignment caused by data quality issues, training inefficiencies, and the lack of well-designed optimization objectives. To address these limitations, we propose a framework named $\textbf{S}$h$\textbf{o}$rt-to-$\textbf{Lo}$ng $\textbf{P}$reference $\textbf{O}$ptimization ($\textbf{SoLoPO}$), decoupling long-context preference optimization (PO) into two components: short-context PO and short-to-long reward alignment (SoLo-RA), supported by both theoretical and empirical evidence. Specifically, short-context PO leverages preference pairs sampled from short contexts to enhance the model's contextual knowledge utilization ability. Meanwhile, SoLo-RA explicitly encourages reward score consistency utilization for the responses when conditioned on both short and long contexts that contain identical task-relevant information. This facilitates transferring the model's ability to handle short contexts into long-context scenarios. SoLoPO is compatible with mainstream preference optimization algorithms, while substantially improving the efficiency of data construction and training processes. Experimental results show that SoLoPO enhances all these algorithms with respect to stronger length and domain generalization abilities across various long-context benchmarks, while achieving notable improvements in both computational and memory efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "SoLoPO: Unlocking Long-Context Capabilities in LLMs via Short-to-Long Preference Optimization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework called Short-to-Long Preference Optimization (SoLoPO) to improve the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to effectively utilize long-context information. The core idea is to decouple long-context preference optimization into two components: short-context preference optimization and short-to-long reward alignment (SoLo-RA). The short-context PO focuses on leveraging preference pairs sampled from shorter contexts to improve contextual knowledge utilization, while SoLo-RA explicitly encourages consistency in reward scores for responses conditioned on both short and long contexts containing identical task-relevant information. SoLoPO is compatible with mainstream preference optimization algorithms (DPO, SimPO, ORPO) and significantly enhances data construction and training efficiency. Experimental results demonstrate improved length and domain generalization abilities across various long-context benchmarks with better computational and memory efficiency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novelty lies in the decoupling of long-context preference optimization into short-context PO and short-to-long reward alignment. This approach provides a new perspective on addressing the long-context alignment problem. The theoretical justification for this decoupling, based on the redundancy hypothesis, is a valuable contribution. The SoLo-RA component, which aims to align reward scores across different context lengths, is also a novel element.

*   **Significance:** Addressing the long-context alignment issue is crucial for the advancement of LLMs and their applicability in real-world scenarios. The paper's contributions in this area are significant. SoLoPO's increased efficiency in data construction and training is a practical benefit, potentially lowering the barrier to entry for improving long-context capabilities. The performance improvements demonstrated on several benchmarks showcase the potential of the proposed framework. Showing compatibility with several PO algorithms increases the significance, making the findings generalizable.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Theoretical Foundation:** The paper provides a theoretical justification for the proposed approach, which strengthens its credibility.
    *   **Practical Implementation:** SoLoPO is readily compatible with existing PO algorithms, making it easy to adopt and integrate.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results demonstrate significant improvements in long-context understanding and generalization abilities, with gains in efficiency and memory usage.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** Testing is done over a varied and comprehensive suite of evaluation settings including LongBench, Nia-plus and open LLM leaderboard,
    *   **Well-written and clear**: The paper explains the approach clearly and precisely so as to be reproducible and applicable to many settings.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset limitations**: The short-to-long dataset is synthesized by adding distractor documents. Though effective, these synthetic long contexts might not fully capture the nuances of real-world long-context scenarios. The realism of the dataset could affect model performance.
    *   **Hyperparameter tuning:** The introduction of new hyperparameters (compression ratio and reward alignment coefficient) can add complexity to the training process. The need for manual tuning of these parameters limits the model's effectiveness in varying settings.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Scope:** The theoretical analysis focuses primarily on input-side considerations and does not directly address challenges related to long-text generation or tasks where output-side dependencies are critical.
    *   **Some limitations in evaluation analysis**: When comparing DPO, SimPO and ORPO across similar conditions, evaluation methods are not kept consistent.

*   **Potential Influence:** SoLoPO has the potential to influence research and development in long-context LLMs. Its decoupled approach and improved efficiency could pave the way for more effective and scalable solutions. By explicitly modeling the connection between short and long contexts, SoLoPO offers a new perspective that can be further explored and extended.

**Rigorous Rationale:**

The SoLoPO paper presents a solid contribution to the field of long-context LLMs, with a novel approach that combines theoretical justification with practical implementation and empirical validation. The decoupling strategy is clever, and the results indicate improved performance and efficiency. However, the limitations of the synthetic long-context data, hyperparameter tuning, and theoretical scope prevent it from being a groundbreaking contribution. It builds upon existing work but offers a valuable and potentially influential advancement.

### CheX-DS: Improving Chest X-ray Image Classification with Ensemble Learning Based on DenseNet and Swin Transformer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11168v1)
- **Authors**: Xinran Li, Yu Liu, Xiujuan Xu, Xiaowei Zhao
- **Abstract**: The automatic diagnosis of chest diseases is a popular and challenging task. Most current methods are based on convolutional neural networks (CNNs), which focus on local features while neglecting global features. Recently, self-attention mechanisms have been introduced into the field of computer vision, demonstrating superior performance. Therefore, this paper proposes an effective model, CheX-DS, for classifying long-tail multi-label data in the medical field of chest X-rays. The model is based on the excellent CNN model DenseNet for medical imaging and the newly popular Swin Transformer model, utilizing ensemble deep learning techniques to combine the two models and leverage the advantages of both CNNs and Transformers. The loss function of CheX-DS combines weighted binary cross-entropy loss with asymmetric loss, effectively addressing the issue of data imbalance. The NIH ChestX-ray14 dataset is selected to evaluate the model's effectiveness. The model outperforms previous studies with an excellent average AUC score of 83.76\%, demonstrating its superior performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "CheX-DS: Improving Chest X-ray Image Classification with Ensemble Learning Based on DenseNet and Swin Transformer" proposes a new model, CheX-DS, for classifying chest X-ray images with a focus on addressing the challenges of long-tail multi-label data. CheX-DS combines a DenseNet and a Swin Transformer using ensemble learning techniques.  The model uses a modified loss function combining weighted binary cross-entropy with asymmetric loss to handle data imbalance. The authors evaluate their model on the NIH ChestX-ray14 dataset and report improved performance in terms of average AUC compared to individual models and other existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Ensemble Approach:** The use of ensemble learning to combine the strengths of CNNs (DenseNet) and Transformers (Swin Transformer) is a reasonable approach. CNNs are good at local feature extraction, while Transformers excel at capturing global dependencies.
*   **Addressing Data Imbalance:** The paper tackles the critical issue of long-tail distribution in medical datasets by employing a combined loss function (weighted binary cross-entropy with asymmetric loss). This is a well-established technique, but its application within the context of this specific ensemble architecture is valuable.
*   **Clear Methodology:** The paper presents a clear description of the model architecture, training process, and evaluation metrics.
*   **Experimental Validation:** The authors conduct experiments on the NIH ChestX-ray14 dataset and compare CheX-DS with both individual models and other state-of-the-art methods.
*   **Demonstrated Performance Improvement:** The paper reports an increase in the average AUC score, demonstrating the potential benefits of the proposed CheX-DS model. The results show improved performance in the "No Finding" category, which is a good addition.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty:** While the ensemble approach and the application of a combined loss function are reasonable, the core components (DenseNet and Swin Transformer) are well-established. The novelty primarily lies in the specific combination and fine-tuning. This is a relatively straightforward application of established methodologies.
*   **Incremental Improvement:** The performance improvement, while present, doesn't appear to be drastically significant compared to other existing approaches. The differences in AUC scores in Table IV seem marginal. This is a key point to be critical about, as significant improvements are necessary to warrant the claim of a new technique as opposed to incremental progress.
*   **Dataset Limitations:** The NIH ChestX-ray14 dataset, while widely used, has limitations. It is known to contain label noise and is not representative of real-world clinical settings.
*   **Lack of Ablation Studies:** While loss functions are compared, there isn't a thorough ablation study to analyze the contribution of different components (e.g., contribution of each network). Ablation studies provide insights on the effectiveness of the different components.
*   **Limited Clinical Relevance Analysis:** While the paper provides AUC values, it lacks a more detailed discussion of the clinical relevance of the improvements. For example, which specific disease diagnostic tasks are significantly improved? How might these improvements impact patient outcomes?

**Significance:**

The paper contributes to the field of chest X-ray image classification by providing an ensemble model that addresses the challenges of data imbalance and multi-label classification. The results show some improvement over existing methods. However, the limited novelty and incremental performance gains restrict the overall significance of the work.

**Justification for the Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6** to this paper. While the paper presents a technically sound approach and demonstrates some performance improvement, it lacks significant novelty. The use of ensemble learning and established network architectures (DenseNet and Swin Transformer) and data-balancing loss function is not groundbreaking. The performance gains, while present, are incremental rather than transformative, and more rigorous testing and ablation studies would be necessary to strengthen the claims. The analysis in this paper can be seen as a good, competent piece of engineering with some adaptation for the application at hand, as opposed to a genuinely insightful and groundbreaking advance.

### Gaussian Weight Sampling for Scalable, Efficient and Stable Pseudo-Quantization Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11170v1)
- **Authors**: Myeonghwan Ahn, Sungjoo Yoo
- **Abstract**: Ever-growing scale of large language models (LLMs) is pushing for improved efficiency, favoring fully quantized training (FQT) over BF16. While FQT accelerates training, it faces consistency challenges and requires searching over an exponential number of cases, each needing over 200B tokens to ensure stability. Pseudo-quantization training (PQT) addresses the issues of FQT, although it is not well-studied. We explore the practical implications of PQT in detail and propose a noise distribution $R$ that is floating-point (FP)-friendly, with ideal properties including stochastic precision annealing. As a result, the proposed method serves as an effective theoretical foundation for low-precision FP parameters through PQT, utilizing efficient fake quantization via an addition and subsequent FP casting. We demonstrate that Gaussian weight sampling is (1) scalable: supports low-precision FP parameters down to FP6 and high-precision noise up to 9-bit with BF16 operator. The proposed method is (2) efficient: incurring computational overhead as low as 1.40\% on the A100 GPU in terms of Llama2 training tokens per second, and requiring 2 bytes per parameter in GPU memory. We demonstrate that PQT with Gaussian weight sampling is (3) stable: closely following or even surpassing performance of the BF16 baseline while pre-training GPT2 and Llama2 models with up to 1B parameters and 300B tokens.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Gaussian Weight Sampling for Scalable, Efficient and Stable Pseudo-Quantization Training":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel pseudo-quantization training (PQT) method called "Gaussian Weight Sampling" (GaussWS) designed to enhance the scalability, efficiency, and stability of training large language models (LLMs) with low-precision floating-point (FP) parameters. The core idea is to use a rounded normal distribution as the basis for the pseudo-quantization noise (PQN), making the method more FP-friendly and offering properties like stochastic precision annealing. The authors demonstrate that GaussWS can support low-precision FP parameters (down to FP6) and high-precision noise, incur low computational overhead, and achieve stable training comparable to or even surpassing BF16 baselines for GPT2 and Llama2 models up to 1B parameters.  Key aspects include efficient noise generation using bitwise operations, ensuring forward-backward consistency, and addressing underflow issues during PQT.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a valuable and incremental improvement on existing PQT methods. While the general concept of PQT is not new (DiffQ and NIPQ already exist), the specific implementation of Gaussian noise for weight sampling, especially the analysis of its impact on FP underflow and stochastic precision annealing, adds a distinct contribution. The detailed analysis relating the noise distribution to precision scaling is valuable.

* **Significance:**  The paper tackles a crucial problem in LLM training: reducing computational costs without sacrificing performance.  The potential impact is significant, as it addresses the growing energy demands and hardware requirements associated with large model training. The efficiency gains (low computational overhead and memory footprint) are compelling. It demonstrates the applicability for large scale models (upto 1B parameters) which is a critical factor. The claims of stability comparable to BF16 are significant as stability is a major hurdle in low precision training.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Thorough Analysis:** The paper provides a comprehensive analysis of PQT in the context of FP datatypes, identifying limitations of existing methods and deriving theoretical conditions for stable training (Lemmas 1 & 2, Propositions 3 & 4).
    * **Efficient Implementation:** The use of bitwise operations for noise generation is a clever approach to minimizing computational overhead.
    * **Empirical Validation:**  The experiments cover multiple models (GPT2, Llama2) and datasets, providing robust evidence for the effectiveness of GaussWS.
    * **Addresses Practical Challenges:**  The paper tackles practical issues like forward-backward consistency and management of the bitwidth parameter.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Improvement:** While valuable, the work is primarily an improvement on existing PQT methods rather than a completely disruptive innovation. The core idea remains within the PQT framework.
    * **Scope of Experiments:** While experiments with 1B parameter model is a good start, scaling to even larger models would further strengthen the work.
    * **Limitations are acknowledged but could be further examined:** Authors acknowledge that "The proposed method is applied only on weight, leaving activation and gradient same as baseline BF16". A deeper dive into why extending this to activation might be problematic or future challenges would have been helpful.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in low-precision training by providing a more refined and practical PQT approach.  The insights into noise distribution and FP underflow are valuable for other researchers in the field. The method could also find adoption in practical LLM training pipelines.

* **Justification for Score:** Given the incremental yet valuable nature of the contribution, the robust empirical validation, and the potential for real-world impact, a score in the upper-middle range is appropriate. The paper addresses a relevant problem, provides a theoretically sound and practically efficient solution, and demonstrates its effectiveness through experiments.  However, it's not a groundbreaking departure from existing techniques, and scaling to much larger models needs future exploration.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Low-Resource Language Processing: An OCR-Driven Summarization and Translation Pipeline
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11177v1)
- **Authors**: Hrishit Madhavi, Jacob Cherian, Yuvraj Khamkar, Dhananjay Bhagat
- **Abstract**: This paper presents an end-to-end suite for multilingual information extraction and processing from image-based documents. The system uses Optical Character Recognition (Tesseract) to extract text in languages such as English, Hindi, and Tamil, and then a pipeline involving large language model APIs (Gemini) for cross-lingual translation, abstractive summarization, and re-translation into a target language. Additional modules add sentiment analysis (TensorFlow), topic classification (Transformers), and date extraction (Regex) for better document comprehension. Made available in an accessible Gradio interface, the current research shows a real-world application of libraries, models, and APIs to close the language gap and enhance access to information in image media across different linguistic environments
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents an end-to-end pipeline for multilingual information extraction and processing, specifically designed for low-resource languages and image-based documents. The system employs Optical Character Recognition (OCR) using Tesseract to extract text from images in languages like English, Hindi, and Tamil. This extracted text is then processed through a pipeline incorporating large language model APIs (specifically Gemini) for cross-lingual translation, abstractive summarization, and re-translation.  The pipeline also includes modules for sentiment analysis (TensorFlow), topic classification (Transformers), and date extraction (Regex). A Gradio interface facilitates user interaction. The research aims to enhance information accessibility from image media across different linguistic environments by leveraging existing libraries, models, and APIs. The paper also mentions experiments and analysis of the pipeline performance, including data visualizations,  performance metrics (accuracy, BLEU, ROUGE, WER, CER) and human evaluations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*
The paper's novelty is somewhat limited. While it integrates several existing tools (Tesseract, Gemini/Google Translate, TensorFlow, Transformers, etc.) into a single pipeline, the individual components are not novel in themselves.  The focus on low-resource languages, particularly Indic languages, is a positive aspect. The specific combination of modules – OCR, followed by sentiment, topic classification, summarization, *and* translation – offers a holistic approach, but this configuration, while useful, is not inherently groundbreaking. The use of LLMs (Gemini) for abstractive summarization and re-translation is a standard approach that is now quite common and may not be innovative. The integration in a Gradio interface offers easier access but is an engineering rather than a research novelty.

*Significance:*
The significance of the paper lies primarily in its practical application. Providing a readily usable pipeline for processing image-based documents in low-resource languages addresses a real-world accessibility problem. The emphasis on Indian languages makes the work relevant to a region with significant linguistic diversity and a need for improved information access. However, the lack of substantial novel contributions to the underlying technologies diminishes its significance from a purely research perspective. There is a potential practical impact, especially within specific regional contexts where access to the combined tools is limited or complex to set up independently. A stronger analysis and evaluation of the pipeline's performance specifically for the target languages would bolster its significance. A comprehensive error analysis on OCR accuracy in low-resource languages and how the pipeline addresses these specific errors (e.g. via post-correction) would also be an important contribution.

*Strengths:*
*   Comprehensive Pipeline: Integrates various NLP tasks into a single workflow.
*   Focus on Low-Resource Languages: Addresses a critical need for under-served linguistic communities.
*   Practical Application: The Gradio interface makes the system user-friendly and accessible.
*   Clear Presentation: The paper is well-structured and easy to follow.

*Weaknesses:*
*   Limited Novelty: Relies heavily on existing technologies and APIs.
*   Insufficient Evaluation: The evaluation could be more in-depth, particularly focusing on edge cases, common errors, and the impact of individual components on the overall pipeline performance and the performance in specific languages.
*   Lack of Detailed Implementation Information: The paper describes components used, however, lacks crucial details on configuration, specific training parameters, or design choices.
*   Weakly Defined Contributions: The paper would greatly benefit from a section outlining specific and measurable contributions.

*Potential Influence:*
The paper's influence will likely be limited to practical applications within the targeted communities if the pipeline can be easily deployed and maintained.  It can serve as a useful starting point for building similar systems or inspire further research into specific components, such as OCR error correction or improved summarization techniques for low-resource languages. However, without significant novel contributions, its broader impact on the field will be moderate.

**Score: 5**

**Rationale:**
The score of 5 reflects the paper's balance of practical utility and limited theoretical novelty. While the end-to-end pipeline addresses a genuine accessibility problem and is tailored to low-resource Indian languages, the individual components and their integration do not present a significant breakthrough. The evaluation section is relatively weak given the ambitious nature of the project. The paper essentially combines multiple established methods into a single processing chain, without introducing fundamental improvements or novel approaches to any of the individual tasks or to the combined architecture. Therefore, while valuable in terms of practical application, its research contribution is moderate at best. A higher score would necessitate more innovation in the algorithms or methods used, or a more rigorous and insightful analysis of results, demonstrating how the specific combination of techniques provides advantages over existing solutions.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 5/10

### CompAlign: Improving Compositional Text-to-Image Generation with a Complex Benchmark and Fine-Grained Feedback
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11178v1)
- **Authors**: Yixin Wan, Kai-Wei Chang
- **Abstract**: State-of-the-art T2I models are capable of generating high-resolution images given textual prompts. However, they still struggle with accurately depicting compositional scenes that specify multiple objects, attributes, and spatial relations. We present CompAlign, a challenging benchmark with an emphasis on assessing the depiction of 3D-spatial relationships, for evaluating and improving models on compositional image generation. CompAlign consists of 900 complex multi-subject image generation prompts that combine numerical and 3D-spatial relationships with varied attribute bindings. Our benchmark is remarkably challenging, incorporating generation tasks with 3+ generation subjects with complex 3D-spatial relationships. Additionally, we propose CompQuest, an interpretable and accurate evaluation framework that decomposes complex prompts into atomic sub-questions, then utilizes a MLLM to provide fine-grained binary feedback on the correctness of each aspect of generation elements in model-generated images. This enables precise quantification of alignment between generated images and compositional prompts. Furthermore, we propose an alignment framework that uses CompQuest's feedback as preference signals to improve diffusion models' compositional image generation abilities. Using adjustable per-image preferences, our method is easily scalable and flexible for different tasks. Evaluation of 9 T2I models reveals that: (1) models remarkable struggle more with compositional tasks with more complex 3D-spatial configurations, and (2) a noticeable performance gap exists between open-source accessible models and closed-source commercial models. Further empirical study on using CompAlign for model alignment yield promising results: post-alignment diffusion models achieve remarkable improvements in compositional accuracy, especially on complex generation tasks, outperforming previous approaches.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CompAlign, a new benchmark for evaluating compositional text-to-image (T2I) generation models. CompAlign focuses on complex scenarios involving multiple objects, 3D spatial relationships, and specific attribute bindings, pushing beyond existing benchmarks that often consider simpler compositions. The authors also present CompQuest, an evaluation framework based on decomposing prompts into atomic questions answered by a Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM), allowing for fine-grained assessment of compositional accuracy. Furthermore, they propose an alignment framework to improve diffusion models by using CompQuest's feedback as preference signals. Experiments evaluate several state-of-the-art T2I models, highlighting their struggles with complex compositions and a performance gap between open-source and closed-source models. The alignment framework demonstrates improvements in open-source model performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers several novel components. First, the CompAlign benchmark indeed appears to be a more challenging and comprehensive test of compositional understanding compared to existing datasets like T2I-CompBench++. The focus on 3D spatial relationships and a higher number of objects addresses limitations in previous work. Second, the CompQuest evaluation framework presents a more interpretable and granular approach to assessing image generation accuracy by decomposing prompts into atomic questions and leveraging MLLMs for feedback. Third, the alignment framework that uses this granular feedback to improve open-source diffusion models also has novelty.

*   **Significance:**  The significance of this work is moderate, but could become high with wider adoption and future work.

    *   **Benchmarking:** CompAlign provides a valuable resource for researchers to quantitatively evaluate and compare the compositional reasoning abilities of T2I models. It exposes the weaknesses of existing models in more realistic and challenging settings.
    *   **Evaluation Framework:** CompQuest offers a more principled and interpretable alternative to purely model-generated scores, offering insights into specific areas where models struggle.
    *   **Alignment:** The alignment framework using MLLM feedback as preference signals has the potential to improve the compositional capabilities of open-source diffusion models, closing the gap with closed-source solutions. However, the results are preliminary.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of current T2I models in compositional generation.
    *   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** CompAlign seems to be a well-constructed and challenging benchmark with a diverse set of scenarios.
    *   **Interpretable Evaluation:** CompQuest provides a valuable breakdown of model performance on different compositional aspects.
    *   **Potential for Improvement:** The proposed alignment framework provides a pathway for improving open-source models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on MLLMs:** The evaluation framework heavily relies on MLLMs, which can be subject to biases and errors. Although the paper decomposes questions to be verifiable with binary feedback, the robustness of this evaluation method could be strengthened by analyzing inter-rater agreement between different MLLMs and/or human raters, assessing the accuracy with which the MLLM determines the correctness of the answer.
    *   **Limited Evaluation of Alignment Framework:** The alignment framework is evaluated only on SD1.5 and SD2 with only limited results. The generalizability to stronger open source diffusion models remains unknown.
    *   **Lack of comparison to human evaluation:** The authors note that partially-correct generated images may be subjective. This motivates the use of an automatic, interpretable framework. Nevertheless, it is still useful to compare the performance of models with CompAlign to the performance of the same models by human evaluators.
    *   **Naturalness analysis:** While the authors include an analysis of the naturalness of images generated by various models, it would be useful to include an evaluation of the naturalness of the images generated using the proposed alignment strategy, to determine whether the alignment strategy improves naturalness.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to impact the development and evaluation of future T2I models by providing a more rigorous test of their compositional understanding. The interpretable evaluation framework and alignment strategy could influence how researchers approach these tasks.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a significant contribution by addressing the shortcomings of existing benchmarks and evaluation methods for compositional T2I generation. The CompAlign benchmark and CompQuest evaluation framework are valuable resources for the research community. However, some weaknesses temper the overall score, most notably the reliance on MLLMs for evaluation and the limited evaluation of alignment framework. Furthermore, the paper could be improved by including an evaluation of the models with CompAlign with human evaluators, comparing the performance of the models after applying the alignment strategy with human evaluators, and the impact of the alignment strategy on image naturalness. A higher score would be justified with more extensive experimentation, a more thorough analysis of potential biases in the MLLM-based evaluation, and/or improved naturalness.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### mmRAG: A Modular Benchmark for Retrieval-Augmented Generation over Text, Tables, and Knowledge Graphs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11180v1)
- **Authors**: Chuan Xu, Qiaosheng Chen, Yutong Feng, Gong Cheng
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for enhancing the capabilities of large language models. However, existing RAG evaluation predominantly focuses on text retrieval and relies on opaque, end-to-end assessments of generated outputs. To address these limitations, we introduce mmRAG, a modular benchmark designed for evaluating multi-modal RAG systems. Our benchmark integrates queries from six diverse question-answering datasets spanning text, tables, and knowledge graphs, which we uniformly convert into retrievable documents. To enable direct, granular evaluation of individual RAG components -- such as the accuracy of retrieval and query routing -- beyond end-to-end generation quality, we follow standard information retrieval procedures to annotate document relevance and derive dataset relevance. We establish baseline performance by evaluating a wide range of RAG implementations on mmRAG.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces mmRAG, a new modular benchmark for evaluating retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems.  Unlike existing benchmarks that primarily focus on text retrieval and end-to-end generation performance, mmRAG is designed to evaluate multi-modal RAG systems operating over text, tables, and knowledge graphs. The key features of mmRAG are: (1) a unified corpus incorporating data from six question-answering datasets spanning text, tables, and knowledge graphs; (2) cross-dataset annotations of relevance labels to evaluate retrieval accuracy; and (3) a modular evaluation approach that supports direct evaluation of query routing, retrieval, and generation components.  The paper presents baseline performance results by evaluating various RAG implementations on mmRAG.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a clear gap in the existing RAG benchmark landscape.  The focus on multi-modality (text, tables, KGs) and modular evaluation is a significant step forward. Most existing benchmarks are heavily skewed towards text-based retrieval and lack the fine-grained annotations needed to diagnose bottlenecks within the RAG pipeline (e.g., whether a failure is due to poor retrieval or poor generation). The inclusion of query routing evaluation is particularly novel and important for optimizing RAG systems in real-world scenarios. The construction of unified retrievable documents from multiple data sources is also a useful contribution.

*   **Significance:** mmRAG has the potential to significantly impact the RAG research community.  The modular evaluation framework enables researchers to develop and optimize individual RAG components more effectively.  The multi-modal data allows for the development of RAG systems that can reason over heterogeneous knowledge sources. The release of the benchmark and codebase will facilitate comparisons between different RAG architectures and algorithms. The detailed statistical analysis provides insights into the dataset characteristics.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing RAG benchmarks.
    *   **Comprehensive Design:** mmRAG is thoughtfully designed to address these limitations, with careful consideration given to data collection, annotation, and evaluation methodology.
    *   **Detailed Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation of baseline RAG models on mmRAG, providing valuable insights into their performance.
    *   **Community Contribution:**  The public release of the mmRAG benchmark and codebase will greatly benefit the research community.
    *   **Modular Design:** Clear separation of components enables targeted evaluations.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Annotation Cost:** The LLM-based annotation process is computationally expensive and may limit the scalability of the benchmark. While the authors describe an ensemble approach to mitigate costs, further optimization in this area is needed.
    *   **Limited Modalities:** The current version of mmRAG only supports text, tables, and knowledge graphs, lacking visual modalities.
    *   **Domain Specificity:** There is no support for domain-specific data splits.
    *   **Reliance on LLMs for Annotation:** While a robust annotation protocol is defined, there is still reliance on LLMs to define the ground truth, which can result in certain biases. A detailed evaluation of potential biases would be beneficial.
    *   **No comprehensive error analysis:** While results are provided, more detail on common retrieval errors would be valuable to guide research.

*   **Potential Influence:** mmRAG is likely to become a widely used benchmark for evaluating multi-modal RAG systems.  It has the potential to drive innovation in RAG research by enabling more targeted development and evaluation of individual RAG components. The detailed statistics and analysis provided in the paper can inform the design of new RAG models and algorithms.

*   **Justification:**
    The paper successfully addresses a recognized and significant deficiency in RAG benchmarking by providing a multimodal and modular evaluation framework. The paper demonstrates comprehensive evaluation by providing diverse datasets, annotations and evaluation results. However, reliance on computationally expensive LLM-based annotation and scope for supporting further modalities in future needs to be considered.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Feasibility with Language Models for Open-World Compositional Zero-Shot Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11181v1)
- **Authors**: Jae Myung Kim, Stephan Alaniz, Cordelia Schmid, Zeynep Akata
- **Abstract**: Humans can easily tell if an attribute (also called state) is realistic, i.e., feasible, for an object, e.g. fire can be hot, but it cannot be wet. In Open-World Compositional Zero-Shot Learning, when all possible state-object combinations are considered as unseen classes, zero-shot predictors tend to perform poorly. Our work focuses on using external auxiliary knowledge to determine the feasibility of state-object combinations. Our Feasibility with Language Model (FLM) is a simple and effective approach that leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to better comprehend the semantic relationships between states and objects. FLM involves querying an LLM about the feasibility of a given pair and retrieving the output logit for the positive answer. To mitigate potential misguidance of the LLM given that many of the state-object compositions are rare or completely infeasible, we observe that the in-context learning ability of LLMs is essential. We present an extensive study identifying Vicuna and ChatGPT as best performing, and we demonstrate that our FLM consistently improves OW-CZSL performance across all three benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach called "Feasibility with Language Model" (FLM) for improving Open-World Compositional Zero-Shot Learning (OW-CZSL).  FLM leverages Large Language Models (LLMs) to determine the feasibility of state-object combinations, which are often problematic in OW-CZSL. The core idea is to query an LLM about the feasibility of a given pair and use the LLM's output logit for the positive answer as a feasibility score. The paper emphasizes the importance of in-context learning, where the LLM is provided with examples of feasible pairs from the training set to better understand the dataset-specific context. The authors show that FLM consistently improves OW-CZSL performance across three benchmark datasets when integrated with existing VLM models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs to assess feasibility is relatively novel within the OW-CZSL domain. Prior work often relies on word embeddings or external knowledge graphs, which have limitations in capturing complex semantic relationships. Using LLMs, especially with in-context learning, allows the model to leverage a much larger and more nuanced understanding of language and world knowledge. The use of prompt engineering to guide the LLM and extract a feasibility score is also a useful contribution.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a key challenge in OW-CZSL: the difficulty of handling infeasible or rare state-object combinations. Improving the handling of these combinations directly translates to better generalization performance.  The experimental results demonstrate consistent improvements over existing methods on multiple benchmarks, suggesting that FLM is a significant step forward. Furthermore, the method is designed to be easily integrated into existing VLM architectures, enhancing its practical applicability.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of feasibility in OW-CZSL and motivates the use of LLMs for addressing it.
    * **Simple and Effective Approach:** FLM is conceptually simple and easy to implement, yet it yields substantial performance improvements.
    * **Thorough Experimental Evaluation:** The paper presents a comprehensive experimental evaluation on three benchmark datasets, comparing FLM with several baselines and analyzing the impact of different design choices.
    * **Ablation studies:** Ablation studies further clarify the impact of each module
    * **Versatility:** The method is versatile and can be applied to different VLM backbones.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **LLM Dependency:** The method's performance is highly dependent on the choice and quality of the LLM. The paper notes the importance of LLMs that offer logit access and hints that an LLM model with high logit access could further improve the performance.
    * **Computational Cost:** Querying an LLM for every state-object combination can be computationally expensive, especially in open-world settings with a large number of potential classes. While the paper addresses performance improvements, the computational cost implications are not deeply explored.
    * **Bias in Seen Classes:** As noted by the authors, the reliance on "seen" classes from the training dataset for in-context learning can introduce bias. The performance and fairness of the method will depend on the quality and representativeness of the training data.
    * **Prompt Engineering:** The reliance on careful prompt engineering can be seen as both a strength and a weakness. While the authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their prompts, finding the optimal prompt for a given dataset may require significant effort and experimentation.
    * **Limited scope:** As noted by the authors, the code only considers the logit value of the word yes. By considering the logit value of all possible output words, the module's performance may improve.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in OW-CZSL and related areas. It demonstrates the promise of leveraging LLMs for reasoning about semantic relationships and feasibility in visual recognition tasks.  It also opens up new avenues for research in prompt engineering and in-context learning for VLMs. The approach can potentially be extended to other compositional learning tasks.

* **Rigor:**  The paper is rigorous in its methodology. However, the results are dependent on the choice of the prompt. Future experiments could expand on a larger number of prompt combinations.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a significant contribution to the OW-CZSL field by introducing a novel and effective method that leverages the power of LLMs to address the feasibility problem. While the approach has some limitations, the thorough experimental evaluation and potential for broader applicability make it a valuable contribution.  The method is relatively simple to implement, which can encourage adoption by other researchers. The work is a solid step in bridging the gap between language models and visual reasoning for better compositional generalization.

### On Next-Token Prediction in LLMs: How End Goals Determine the Consistency of Decoding Algorithms
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11183v1)
- **Authors**: Jacob Trauger, Ambuj Tewari
- **Abstract**: Probabilistic next-token prediction trained using cross-entropy loss is the basis of most large language models. Given a sequence of previous values, next-token prediction assigns a probability to each possible next value in the vocabulary. There are many ways to use next-token prediction to output token sequences. This paper examines a few of these algorithms (greedy, lookahead, random sampling, and temperature-scaled random sampling) and studies their consistency with respect to various goals encoded as loss functions. Although consistency of surrogate losses with respect to a target loss function is a well researched topic, we are the first to study it in the context of LLMs (to the best of our knowledge). We find that, so long as next-token prediction converges to its true probability distribution, random sampling is consistent with outputting sequences that mimic sampling from the true probability distribution. For the other goals, such as minimizing the 0-1 loss on the entire sequence, we show no polynomial-time algorithm is optimal for all probability distributions and all decoding algorithms studied are only optimal for a subset of probability distributions. When analyzing these results, we see that there is a dichotomy created between the goals of information retrieval and creative generation for the decoding algorithms. This shows that choosing the correct decoding algorithm based on the desired goal is extremely important and many of the ones used are lacking theoretical grounding in numerous scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the consistency of various decoding algorithms used with large language models (LLMs) that are trained on next-token prediction using the cross-entropy loss function. It analyzes the consistency of greedy, lookahead, random sampling, and temperature-scaled random sampling algorithms with respect to different target loss functions representing different goals. It examines two primary goals: information retrieval (using N-gram Hamming loss) and creative generation (using cross-entropy loss for the entire sequence). The key findings include: deterministic decoding algorithms are necessary for consistency with N-gram Hamming loss, but they perform poorly for cross-entropy loss. Random sampling is consistent for cross-entropy loss but inconsistent for N-gram Hamming loss.  The paper shows the lack of a universal decoding algorithm and highlights the need to tailor the choice of decoding algorithm to the intended task. Further, it shows that polynomial-time algorithms are not always optimal for the N-gram Hamming loss and characterizes when these algorithms are consistent. Finally, it provides a rate for the suboptimality gap of temperature scaling.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in formally analyzing the consistency of decoding algorithms in LLMs, a topic that has received relatively less attention compared to the training and architecture of LLMs themselves. While the concept of surrogate loss consistency is well-established in machine learning, its application to LLM decoding algorithms and their varying goals is relatively novel. It's one of the first to study this in the context of LLMs, setting it apart from existing works.
*   **Significance:** The results are significant because they provide a theoretical underpinning for practical observations about the behavior of different decoding algorithms. It highlights why certain decoding strategies work better for specific tasks. By framing decoding as a surrogate loss consistency problem, it provides a formal lens through which to analyze and potentially improve decoding algorithms in the future. The dichotomy identified between algorithms suitable for information retrieval and creative generation sheds light on the importance of task-specific decoding choices.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Formal and rigorous analysis with proofs.
    *   Clear framing of the problem as a surrogate loss consistency.
    *   Identifies a key dichotomy in goals (retrieval vs. generation).
    *   Provides a framework for analyzing other decoding algorithms.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The strong assumptions, especially Assumption 3.1 (convergence of next-token predictors to true distribution), although a standard assumption, limit the practical applicability somewhat as current models rarely, if ever, truly reach this stage.
    *   The characterization of probability distributions where KT-lookahead is optimal, while theoretically interesting, might be difficult to apply directly in practice due to the complexity of verifying whether a distribution belongs to the defined class *P*.
    *   The simulation study, while helpful, uses simple Markov chains and may not fully capture the complexities of real-world LLMs and text distributions.
    *   The focus is primarily on asymptotic behavior, which might not always translate to the finite-data and compute regime that LLMs operate in.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research on LLM decoding by encouraging more formal analysis and development of task-specific decoding algorithms. It also raises questions about the suitability of current decoding methods for different applications and may spur development of adaptive decoding strategies.
*   **Justification:** While the results are important and the paper presents a rigorous theoretical framework, the strong assumptions and the somewhat limited practical applicability of some results temper the score. The novelty is clear, but the impact is still to be seen.
