### Can Global XAI Methods Reveal Injected Bias in LLMs? SHAP vs Rule Extraction vs RuleSHAP
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11189v1)
- **Authors**: Francesco Sovrano
- **Abstract**: Generative AI systems can help spread information but also misinformation and biases, potentially undermining the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). Explainable AI (XAI) aims to reveal the inner workings of AI systems and expose misbehaviours or biases. However, current XAI tools, built for simpler models, struggle to handle the non-numerical nature of large language models (LLMs). This paper examines the effectiveness of global XAI methods, such as rule-extraction algorithms and SHAP, in detecting bias in LLMs. To do so, we first show a text-to-ordinal mapping strategy to convert non-numerical inputs/outputs into numerical features, enabling these tools to identify (some) misinformation-related biases in LLM-generated content. Then, we inject non-linear biases of varying complexity (univariate, conjunctive, and non-convex) into widespread LLMs like ChatGPT and Llama via system instructions, using global XAI methods to detect them. This way, we found that RuleFit struggles with conjunctive and non-convex biases, while SHAP can approximate conjunctive biases but cannot express them as actionable rules. Hence, we introduce RuleSHAP, a global rule extraction algorithm combining SHAP and RuleFit to detect more non-univariate biases, improving injected bias detection over RuleFit by +94% (MRR@1) on average.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of detecting injected biases in Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically focusing on biases that could hinder progress towards the UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The authors propose a novel framework, RULESHAP, that combines SHAP values and RuleFit to extract more accurate and interpretable rules representing these biases. The approach involves transforming textual data into numerical, ordinal features, injecting biases via system instructions, and evaluating the effectiveness of various XAI methods in detecting these biases.  The paper demonstrates that RULESHAP outperforms existing methods like RuleFit and SHAP in identifying complex, non-linear biases in LLMs like ChatGPT and Llama, improving detection rates by a significant margin.  The authors provide a replication package and conduct ablation studies to validate their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the integration of SHAP values within the RuleFit framework to create RULESHAP. This combines the strengths of both approaches: SHAP's robust feature attribution and RuleFit's interpretable rule extraction. The text-to-ordinal mapping strategy, although not entirely novel in itself, is well-motivated in the context of LLM bias detection and allows for the application of existing XAI tools on non-numerical data. The formulation of different bias complexities (univariate, conjunctive, non-convex) is also a good contribution, providing a structured way to evaluate XAI methods. The idea of using the LLM itself as a judge for certain input/output features is clever, but relies heavily on the assumption that the LLM's "opinion" is a valid indicator of bias-relevant properties.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a relevant and important problem: bias in LLMs, particularly concerning the SDGs.  The ability to detect and understand these biases is crucial for ensuring that LLMs contribute positively to societal goals. The experimental results demonstrating the superiority of RULESHAP over existing methods suggest a practical improvement in bias detection capabilities. The provision of a replication package enhances the reproducibility and accessibility of the work. A weakness is that injected biases, while controlled, may not fully represent real-world biases, limiting the generalizability of the findings. However, the methodology provides a valuable framework for future research in this area. The scale of the experiment, while respectable, could be expanded to include more models, SDG domains, and more diverse and complex injected biases to further validate the effectiveness of RULESHAP in different scenarios. The correlation analysis provides some validation of the text-to-ordinal abstractions, but it might benefit from stronger justification or exploration of alternative abstraction strategies.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Novel integration of SHAP and RuleFit.
    *   Structured approach to bias injection and evaluation.
    *   Strong experimental results demonstrating improved performance.
    *   Replication package for reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Injected biases may not fully represent real-world biases.
    *   Reliance on LLM's "opinion" for feature mapping requires careful validation.
    *   Limited number of LLMs and SDG domains considered.
    *   The "Interpretability" achieved by rule extraction may still be complex for non-experts.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research in XAI for LLMs, particularly in the area of bias detection. The RULESHAP algorithm could be adopted and further developed by other researchers. The proposed framework provides a valuable methodology for evaluating XAI methods in the context of LLMs. The focus on SDG-related biases highlights the importance of addressing ethical considerations in AI development.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and technically sound approach to an important problem. The integration of SHAP and RuleFit is a significant contribution, and the experimental results are compelling. However, the limitations regarding the generalizability of the injected biases and the scale of the experiments prevent a higher score. Nevertheless, the paper represents a valuable contribution to the field and provides a solid foundation for future research.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### DiCo: Revitalizing ConvNets for Scalable and Efficient Diffusion Modeling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11196v1)
- **Authors**: Yuang Ai, Qihang Fan, Xuefeng Hu, Zhenheng Yang, Ran He, Huaibo Huang
- **Abstract**: Diffusion Transformer (DiT), a promising diffusion model for visual generation, demonstrates impressive performance but incurs significant computational overhead. Intriguingly, analysis of pre-trained DiT models reveals that global self-attention is often redundant, predominantly capturing local patterns-highlighting the potential for more efficient alternatives. In this paper, we revisit convolution as an alternative building block for constructing efficient and expressive diffusion models. However, naively replacing self-attention with convolution typically results in degraded performance. Our investigations attribute this performance gap to the higher channel redundancy in ConvNets compared to Transformers. To resolve this, we introduce a compact channel attention mechanism that promotes the activation of more diverse channels, thereby enhancing feature diversity. This leads to Diffusion ConvNet (DiCo), a family of diffusion models built entirely from standard ConvNet modules, offering strong generative performance with significant efficiency gains. On class-conditional ImageNet benchmarks, DiCo outperforms previous diffusion models in both image quality and generation speed. Notably, DiCo-XL achieves an FID of 2.05 at 256x256 resolution and 2.53 at 512x512, with a 2.7x and 3.1x speedup over DiT-XL/2, respectively. Furthermore, our largest model, DiCo-H, scaled to 1B parameters, reaches an FID of 1.90 on ImageNet 256x256-without any additional supervision during training. Code: https://github.com/shallowdream204/DiCo.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "DiCo: Revitalizing ConvNets for Scalable and Efficient Diffusion Modeling":

**Summary:**

The paper "DiCo: Revitalizing ConvNets for Scalable and Efficient Diffusion Modeling" addresses the computational overhead associated with Diffusion Transformer (DiT) models in image generation.  The authors observe that self-attention in pre-trained DiT models often focuses on local patterns, suggesting redundancy in global attention computation. They propose Diffusion ConvNet (DiCo), a fully convolutional architecture for diffusion models. To overcome the performance degradation typically observed when replacing self-attention with convolution, DiCo incorporates a "compact channel attention" (CCA) mechanism to promote feature diversity and reduce channel redundancy within the convolutional layers.  Experiments on ImageNet demonstrate that DiCo achieves state-of-the-art image quality with significant efficiency gains compared to DiT and other alternative diffusion models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The novelty of the paper lies in the combination of several elements:

*   **Revisiting ConvNets:** While not entirely new, the renewed focus on ConvNets for diffusion models, especially given the Transformer dominance, is a noteworthy aspect. The authors challenge the prevailing trend.
*   **Identifying Redundancy in DiT Attention:**  The analysis of pre-trained DiT models' attention maps to reveal local dominance is a crucial observation that motivates the design choices. This helps justify the return to convnets.
*   **Compact Channel Attention (CCA):** The CCA mechanism is the core technical contribution. It's a specific design choice tailored to address the limitations of ConvNets when directly substituted for Transformers in diffusion models. It is a well designed, lightweight attention that promotes feature diversity.
*   **Streamlined ConvNet Architecture:** Using mostly 1x1 and 3x3 convolutions for a simple and efficient design for DiCo.

**Significance:**

*   **Improved Efficiency:** The paper demonstrably improves the efficiency of diffusion models, which is crucial for scalability and practical deployment, especially for high-resolution image generation. The provided speedup numbers are compelling.
*   **Strong Generative Performance:** DiCo achieves competitive, even state-of-the-art, FID scores on ImageNet, proving that ConvNets, when appropriately designed, can be a powerful alternative to Transformers in diffusion models.
*   **Hardware-Friendly Design:**  ConvNets are generally more hardware-friendly than Transformers, which could lead to wider accessibility and faster deployment on resource-constrained platforms.
*   **Relevance to the Field:** This work challenges the assumption that Transformers are the only viable architecture for diffusion models and encourages further exploration of alternative, efficient designs.

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-Motivated Design:** The paper clearly motivates its design choices with empirical observations about attention patterns in DiT models.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The authors provide extensive experimental results on ImageNet, comparing DiCo against a wide range of baseline models.
*   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies effectively demonstrate the contribution of each component of DiCo, particularly the CCA mechanism.
*   **Clear and Concise Writing:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow, even for readers not intimately familiar with diffusion models.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The study is primarily focused on class-conditional ImageNet generation.  It doesn't explore text-to-image generation or other generative tasks.
*   **Incremental Improvement:**  While DiCo achieves strong performance, the improvements over existing DiT models are incremental, rather than revolutionary.
*   **Reliance on Pre-trained VAE:** The models rely on a pre-trained VAE encoder. Performance gains may be different when trained end-to-end.
*   **Limited Analysis on other Datasets:** The analysis on Imagenet datasets might not translate to more diverse and complex datasets.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper is likely to have a positive influence on the field by:

*   Inspiring further research into efficient architectures for diffusion models, including ConvNets and other alternatives to Transformers.
*   Motivating a deeper understanding of the role of attention in generative tasks.
*   Providing a practical and effective approach for building high-performance convolutional diffusion models.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** to this paper.

*   The work offers a valuable contribution to the field by revisiting and revitalizing ConvNets for diffusion modeling. The demonstrated improvements in efficiency and strong generative performance are significant.
*   The motivation is clearly stated, with strong empirical evidence.
*   The experiments and results support the authors' claims.

However, the improvements are incremental, rather than transformative, and the study's scope is somewhat limited. While well-executed, the core idea is not entirely groundbreaking. The work is significant enough to be well-received and influence future research.

### NoPE: The Counting Power of Transformers with No Positional Encodings
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11199v1)
- **Authors**: Chris Köcher, Alexander Kozachinskiy, Anthony Widjaja Lin, Marco Sälzer, Georg Zetzsche
- **Abstract**: Positional Encodings (PEs) seem to be indispensable for ensuring expressiveness of transformers; without them attention transformers reduce to a bag-of-word model. NoPE-transformers (i.e. with No PEs) with unique hard attention mechanisms were very recently shown to only be able to express regular languages, i.e., with limited counting ability. This paper shows that, with average hard attention mechanisms, NoPE-transformers are still surprisingly expressive: they can express counting languages corresponding to nonnegative integer solutions to multivariate polynomial equations (i.e. Diophantine equations), reasoning about which is well-known to be undecidable. In fact, we provide a precise characterization of languages expressible by Average Hard Attention NoPE-Transformers (NoPE-AHATs): they correspond precisely to what we call \emph{semi-algebraic sets}, i.e., finite unions of sets of nonnegative integer solutions to systems of multivariate polynomial inequations. We obtain several interesting consequences of our characterization. Firstly, NoPE-transformers can express counting properties that are far more complex than established models like simplified counter machines and Petri nets, but cannot express a very simple counting property of PARITY. Secondly, the problem of analyzing NoPE-transformers is undecidable, e.g., whether a given NoPE transformer classifies all input strings in one class. To complement our results, we exhibit a counting language that is not expressible by average hard attention transformers even with arbitrary PEs but is expressible in the circuit complexity class TC$^0$, answering an open problem.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "NoPE: The Counting Power of Transformers with No Positional Encodings":

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the expressive power of attention transformers *without* positional encodings (NoPE-transformers), focusing on those employing average hard attention (AHAT).  The central result is that NoPE-AHATs can express counting languages corresponding to semi-algebraic sets, i.e., finite unions of sets of nonnegative integer solutions to systems of multivariate polynomial inequalities (Diophantine equations). This significantly contrasts with unique hard attention transformers, which can only express regular languages.  The authors demonstrate several consequences of this expressiveness, including the ability to express counting properties more complex than those handled by simplified counter machines or Petri nets, the undecidability of analyzing NoPE-transformers, and the surprising inability to express PARITY without positional encodings. The paper also exhibits a permutation-invariant counting language outside the scope of average hard attention transformers even with arbitrary positional encoding, placing AHAT strictly within the circuit complexity class TC⁰, solving an open problem.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The primary result – the characterization of NoPE-AHATs' expressive power in terms of semi-algebraic sets – is novel and represents a substantial contribution. The paper tackles a well-defined and previously open problem in the theoretical understanding of transformers. The characterization provides a powerful tool for analyzing and comparing the expressiveness of transformers with different architectures and attention mechanisms. The connection to Diophantine equations, with its inherent undecidability implications, is particularly striking. Answering the open question relating AHAT to TCº is an excellent addition.
*   **Significance:** The findings are significant for several reasons:
    *   **Theoretical Understanding:** It deepens our theoretical comprehension of transformers, clarifying the role of positional encodings and the impact of different attention mechanisms on expressive power.
    *   **Model Comparison:** The paper provides a framework for comparing the expressive capabilities of transformers to other established computational models, such as counter machines and Petri nets.
    *   **Undecidability:** Establishing the undecidability of analyzing NoPE-transformers has practical implications for formal verification and robustness checking efforts.
    *   **Circuit Complexity:**  The results on separating AHAT from TC⁰ closes an important gap in the understanding of the circuit complexity class of AHAT.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Proofs:** The paper seems to contain rigorous proofs.
    *   **Clear Characterization:** The semi-algebraic characterization is clear and concise, providing a direct connection to well-established mathematical concepts.
    *   **Well-defined scope:** Focusing on average hard attention makes the paper tractable and permits well defined results.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The theoretical nature of the results might limit their immediate impact on practical transformer architectures. While AHAT is a reasonable approximation, it's not a precise match for how most transformers operate in practice.
    *   **AHAT is an approximation:** AHAT is an approximation of true behavior of transformers, meaning this results will always have to be interpreted with caution in real world scenarios.
    *   **High Level Approach:** The focus on high level language definitions doesn't necessarily provide much insight into the *how* transformers learn.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future theoretical work on transformers, guiding research on expressiveness, trainability, and verification. The characterization of NoPE-AHATs could be used as a benchmark for other transformer architectures. The open question of which counting languages can be expressed by AHATs even *with* positional encoding is well defined and an important future avenue.

**Score:** 8

**Rationale:**
The paper tackles a significant, open problem in transformer theory and provides a novel and rigorous characterization of the expressive power of NoPE-AHATs. The undecidability implications and the connection to circuit complexity class TC⁰ are particularly interesting. While the theoretical focus might limit immediate practical impact, the results substantially advance our understanding of transformers and provide a foundation for future research. Therefore, it merits a high score.
Score: 8

- **Score**: 8/10

### Audio Turing Test: Benchmarking the Human-likeness of Large Language Model-based Text-to-Speech Systems in Chinese
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11200v1)
- **Authors**: Xihuai Wang, Ziyi Zhao, Siyu Ren, Shao Zhang, Song Li, Xiaoyu Li, Ziwen Wang, Lin Qiu, Guanglu Wan, Xuezhi Cao, Xunliang Cai, Weinan Zhang
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have significantly improved text-to-speech (TTS) systems, enhancing control over speech style, naturalness, and emotional expression, which brings TTS Systems closer to human-level performance. Although the Mean Opinion Score (MOS) remains the standard for TTS System evaluation, it suffers from subjectivity, environmental inconsistencies, and limited interpretability. Existing evaluation datasets also lack a multi-dimensional design, often neglecting factors such as speaking styles, context diversity, and trap utterances, which is particularly evident in Chinese TTS evaluation. To address these challenges, we introduce the Audio Turing Test (ATT), a multi-dimensional Chinese corpus dataset ATT-Corpus paired with a simple, Turing-Test-inspired evaluation protocol. Instead of relying on complex MOS scales or direct model comparisons, ATT asks evaluators to judge whether a voice sounds human. This simplification reduces rating bias and improves evaluation robustness. To further support rapid model development, we also finetune Qwen2-Audio-Instruct with human judgment data as Auto-ATT for automatic evaluation. Experimental results show that ATT effectively differentiates models across specific capability dimensions using its multi-dimensional design. Auto-ATT also demonstrates strong alignment with human evaluations, confirming its value as a fast and reliable assessment tool. The white-box ATT-Corpus and Auto-ATT can be found in ATT Hugging Face Collection (https://huggingface.co/collections/meituan/audio-turing-test-682446320368164faeaf38a4).
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**
The paper introduces the Audio Turing Test (ATT), a new evaluation framework designed to benchmark the human-likeness of Chinese Text-to-Speech (TTS) systems, particularly those based on large language models (LLMs). The ATT framework comprises two main components: a multi-dimensional Chinese corpus (ATT-Corpus) that addresses key challenges in Chinese speech synthesis, and a Turing-Test-inspired evaluation protocol where evaluators judge whether a voice sounds human. The paper also presents Auto-ATT, an automatic evaluation tool fine-tuned from Qwen2-Audio-Instruct, to enable faster model iteration. Experimental results demonstrate that ATT effectively differentiates between TTS models across various capability dimensions and that Auto-ATT aligns well with human evaluations. The ATT-Corpus and Auto-ATT are released publicly.

**Critical Evaluation:**
The paper addresses a significant problem in TTS evaluation: the limitations of MOS (Mean Opinion Score) and existing evaluation datasets in accurately assessing the human-likeness of modern LLM-based TTS systems, especially in Chinese. The ATT framework offers several strengths. The multi-dimensional ATT-Corpus, designed to target specific challenges in Chinese TTS (special characters, code-switching, etc.), is a valuable contribution. The Turing-Test-inspired evaluation protocol offers a simpler, potentially more robust method compared to traditional MOS, reducing bias. The development of Auto-ATT is also a significant step towards faster and more efficient TTS model development.

However, several aspects require critical consideration:

*   **Novelty:** While the idea of using a Turing Test-inspired approach isn't entirely new, its specific application to Chinese TTS systems with a focus on multi-dimensional evaluation and automatic assessment tool (Auto-ATT) is a relatively novel.
*   **Significance:** The ATT framework holds the potential to significantly impact the field. It provides a more nuanced way to evaluate TTS systems, leading to better model development and ultimately, more human-like speech synthesis. It also offers a practical solution for faster evaluation through Auto-ATT.
*   **Limitations:** The paper acknowledges the language-specific nature of the ATT framework, which currently limits its generalizability. The framework's reliance on human evaluators, even with the simplified protocol, can still introduce subjectivity, although it appears reduced compared to MOS. The paper would benefit from further exploration into why their HLS score are much lower and contradict other MOS scores.
*   **Rigor:** The paper presents a detailed methodology, including corpus generation, evaluation protocol, and model training. The statistical significance tests performed on the evaluation protocol is also a strength. The experiments demonstrating the effectiveness of ATT and Auto-ATT are well-designed. However, the explanation of the exact architecture of Qwen2 and its adaptation into Auto-ATT could be expanded.
*   **Impact:** The impact of the paper will depend on the community's adoption of the ATT framework and the quality of the released corpus and Auto-ATT tool. The open-sourcing of resources should encourage broader use and further development.

Despite its language-specific focus, the ATT framework offers a valuable and novel approach to address a real problem in TTS evaluation. It offers a more sensitive assessment method and encourages further innovation in TTS system development. The provision of Auto-ATT accelerates the development process and allows for more testing of TTS models.
