### GuideBench: Benchmarking Domain-Oriented Guideline Following for LLM Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11368v1)
- **Authors**: Lingxiao Diao, Xinyue Xu, Wanxuan Sun, Cheng Yang, Zhuosheng Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have been widely deployed as autonomous agents capable of following user instructions and making decisions in real-world applications. Previous studies have made notable progress in benchmarking the instruction following capabilities of LLMs in general domains, with a primary focus on their inherent commonsense knowledge. Recently, LLMs have been increasingly deployed as domain-oriented agents, which rely on domain-oriented guidelines that may conflict with their commonsense knowledge. These guidelines exhibit two key characteristics: they consist of a wide range of domain-oriented rules and are subject to frequent updates. Despite these challenges, the absence of comprehensive benchmarks for evaluating the domain-oriented guideline following capabilities of LLMs presents a significant obstacle to their effective assessment and further development. In this paper, we introduce GuideBench, a comprehensive benchmark designed to evaluate guideline following performance of LLMs. GuideBench evaluates LLMs on three critical aspects: (i) adherence to diverse rules, (ii) robustness to rule updates, and (iii) alignment with human preferences. Experimental results on a range of LLMs indicate substantial opportunities for improving their ability to follow domain-oriented guidelines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper abstract and introduction:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GUIDEBENCH, a benchmark designed to evaluate the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to follow domain-oriented guidelines, particularly within the context of autonomous agents. Unlike existing benchmarks that focus on general instruction following and commonsense knowledge, GUIDEBENCH addresses the challenges posed by domain-specific rules, which can be complex, frequently updated, and potentially conflict with LLMs' pre-existing knowledge. The benchmark evaluates LLMs on adherence to diverse rules, robustness to rule updates, and alignment with human preferences. The experimental results demonstrate that LLMs often struggle with these domain-oriented guidelines, highlighting opportunities for improvement. The benchmark includes 1272 instances across 7 categories, offering both multiple-choice and question-answering tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in specifically targeting domain-oriented guideline following, an area that's becoming increasingly relevant as LLMs are deployed in real-world applications requiring adherence to specific rules and regulations. Existing benchmarks largely overlook this aspect, making GUIDEBENCH a timely and valuable contribution.

*   **Significance:** GUIDEBENCH addresses a significant gap in LLM evaluation. By focusing on domain-specific rules that may conflict with commonsense, the benchmark identifies weaknesses in LLMs' ability to adapt to real-world constraints. The benchmark can provide valuable insights for improving the reliability and trustworthiness of LLM-based agents used in areas such as operations, incident management, auditing, and other rule-based workflows. Its evaluation of robustness to rule updates and alignment with human preference is also very valuable.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Focus on a practical and increasingly important problem: LLMs adhering to real-world regulations.
    *   Comprehensive benchmark design: Evaluating multiple facets of guideline adherence (rule adherence, rule updates, human preferences).
    *   Substantial dataset: 1272 instances across 7 categories provides a good breadth for evaluating.
    *   The use of both multiple-choice and question-answering tasks provides a good mix of task types.
    *   The identified task categories are relevant to the application of autonomous agents in real-world scenarios.
    *   The paper mentions automatic synthesis and human-in-the-loop refinement which helps improve the reliability of the data generation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   From the abstract and introduction alone, it's difficult to fully assess the quality and comprehensiveness of the domain-specific guidelines used in GUIDEBENCH. The paper mentions automation and human refinement which are good signs but without seeing the full benchmark it is hard to assign a high score.
    *   The reliance on GPT-4 for parsing model responses, as mentioned, suggests some models may struggle even to produce analyzable outputs. While the GPT-4 parsing normalizes outputs, it could mask underlying problems in the model.
    *   The limited performance numbers in the introduction and abstract, make it hard to gauge the magnitude of improvements needed.
    *   The paper does not discuss the impact on computational cost for this domain-specific evaluation. LLMs can be very resource intensive, and a focus on resource conservation should be a criterion.

*   **Potential Influence:** GUIDEBENCH has the potential to become a widely used benchmark for evaluating LLMs in specific domain applications, driving research in rule-following capabilities, and improving the reliability of LLM-based agents. The detailed analysis provided by the benchmark could guide the development of more robust and adaptable LLMs.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:** GUIDEBENCH tackles an important, emerging problem in LLM evaluation, and its design appears well-suited to addressing the challenges of domain-oriented guideline following. The focus on real-world rules, robustness to change, and alignment with human preferences is significant. However, the lack of more specific information about the benchmark itself, and the heavy reliance on GPT-4 for evaluation tempers the assessment. Without seeing the data, annotation process, and thorough exploration of the results, a higher score can't be justified. That said, if the benchmark lives up to its promise, the paper could have a considerable impact on the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### IISE PG&E Energy Analytics Challenge 2025: Hourly-Binned Regression Models Beat Transformers in Load Forecasting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11390v1)
- **Authors**: Millend Roy, Vladimir Pyltsov, Yinbo Hu
- **Abstract**: Accurate electricity load forecasting is essential for grid stability, resource optimization, and renewable energy integration. While transformer-based deep learning models like TimeGPT have gained traction in time-series forecasting, their effectiveness in long-term electricity load prediction remains uncertain. This study evaluates forecasting models ranging from classical regression techniques to advanced deep learning architectures using data from the ESD 2025 competition. The dataset includes two years of historical electricity load data, alongside temperature and global horizontal irradiance (GHI) across five sites, with a one-day-ahead forecasting horizon. Since actual test set load values remain undisclosed, leveraging predicted values would accumulate errors, making this a long-term forecasting challenge. We employ (i) Principal Component Analysis (PCA) for dimensionality reduction and (ii) frame the task as a regression problem, using temperature and GHI as covariates to predict load for each hour, (iii) ultimately stacking 24 models to generate yearly forecasts. Our results reveal that deep learning models, including TimeGPT, fail to consistently outperform simpler statistical and machine learning approaches due to the limited availability of training data and exogenous variables. In contrast, XGBoost, with minimal feature engineering, delivers the lowest error rates across all test cases while maintaining computational efficiency. This highlights the limitations of deep learning in long-term electricity forecasting and reinforces the importance of model selection based on dataset characteristics rather than complexity. Our study provides insights into practical forecasting applications and contributes to the ongoing discussion on the trade-offs between traditional and modern forecasting methods.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the provided paper. Here's a summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of electricity load forecasting, specifically in a constrained environment provided by the IISE PG&E Energy Analytics Challenge 2025. The challenge involves predicting hourly electricity load one-day-ahead, given only two years of historical load data, temperature, and global horizontal irradiance (GHI) from five sites. The key constraint is the absence of actual test set load values, preventing the use of autoregressive components in models, making it a long-term forecasting challenge. The authors compare various forecasting methodologies, ranging from simple regression techniques to advanced deep learning architectures, including TimeGPT.  They employ PCA for dimensionality reduction of exogenous variables and frame the problem as a regression task, training 24 independent hourly models and stacking them. The results surprisingly indicate that simpler models, especially XGBoost, outperform complex deep learning methods in this constrained setting. The paper emphasizes the importance of model selection based on dataset characteristics rather than complexity, especially when data is limited. They also explore the limited impact of adding lagged features of exogenous variables in this context.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the *empirical* comparison of different forecasting methods, including TimeGPT, under the *specific constraints* imposed by the ESD 2025 competition. While the individual methods themselves are well-established, the side-by-side evaluation highlighting the superior performance of XGBoost over deep learning architectures in a *constrained, long-term forecasting scenario* adds a practical insight to the field. The implementation of Principal Component Analysis as part of their method and the utilization of hour-by-hour predictions are useful for the domain.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its practical implications for electricity load forecasting. It reinforces the idea that complex deep learning models are *not always* the best choice, especially when data is limited, feature engineering opportunities are restricted, and long-term predictions are required without autoregressive components. The findings are valuable for practitioners who must often make decisions based on available data and computational resources. By demonstrating that XGBoost, with minimal feature engineering, can outperform more sophisticated models, the paper encourages a more pragmatic approach to model selection. The paper’s focus on limited datasets makes it particularly relevant for real-world problems.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Systematic Comparison:** The paper provides a systematic and well-documented comparison of a wide range of forecasting methods.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The methodology, including the use of PCA and the hourly model stacking approach, is clearly explained.
    *   **Emphasis on Constraints:** The paper effectively highlights the importance of considering problem constraints when selecting a forecasting model.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The findings have practical implications for real-world electricity load forecasting.
    *   **Analysis of TimeGPT** The analysis of why TimeGPT performed poorly is important for practical real-world implications.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The results may be specific to the dataset and constraints of the ESD 2025 competition.  The lack of information regarding the specific years for which the data was provided impacts its generalizability. Furthermore, the findings might not hold in scenarios with abundant data, autoregressive information, or richer feature sets. The models that had high computational intensity with limited results should have been discussed further.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Feature Engineering:** While the paper explores lagged exogenous variables, the feature engineering could have been more in-depth. The paper only considered the first lag variable, and it’s unclear if more investigation into the other PCA components might have been beneficial.
    *   **Lack of hyperparameter tuning analysis** There isn't significant discussion of the hyperparameter tuning performed on all models.
    *   **Focus on XGBoost:** It may be worthwhile to highlight how the architecture of XGBoost can outperform the black-box nature of pre-trained LLMs like TimeGPT.

*   **Score:** 7/10

*   **Justification:**
    The paper makes a useful contribution by highlighting the effectiveness of simpler models in a constrained forecasting setting. While the individual techniques are not novel, the *comparative empirical evaluation* is valuable and has practical relevance. However, the *limited generalizability* of the findings and the scope of analysis (especially concerning feature engineering and deeper exploration of model architectures within the given constraints) prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. Thus, it scores 7, indicating a solid contribution with practical value but limited theoretical novelty and some limitations in its scope and generalizability.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### LipDiffuser: Lip-to-Speech Generation with Conditional Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11391v1)
- **Authors**: Danilo de Oliveira, Julius Richter, Tal Peer, Timo Germann
- **Abstract**: We present LipDiffuser, a conditional diffusion model for lip-to-speech generation synthesizing natural and intelligible speech directly from silent video recordings. Our approach leverages the magnitude-preserving ablated diffusion model (MP-ADM) architecture as a denoiser model. To effectively condition the model, we incorporate visual features using magnitude-preserving feature-wise linear modulation (MP-FiLM) alongside speaker embeddings. A neural vocoder then reconstructs the speech waveform from the generated mel-spectrograms. Evaluations on LRS3 and TCD-TIMIT demonstrate that LipDiffuser outperforms existing lip-to-speech baselines in perceptual speech quality and speaker similarity, while remaining competitive in downstream automatic speech recognition (ASR). These findings are also supported by a formal listening experiment. Extensive ablation studies and cross-dataset evaluation confirm the effectiveness and generalization capabilities of our approach.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the LipDiffuser paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LipDiffuser, a conditional diffusion model designed to generate speech from silent video recordings of lip movements, conditioned also on speaker embeddings. The approach leverages the magnitude-preserving ablated diffusion model (MP-ADM) architecture as the denoiser and integrates visual features through magnitude-preserving feature-wise linear modulation (MP-FiLM) layers within the network's decoder. The system uses a neural vocoder to reconstruct the final speech waveform. The authors evaluate LipDiffuser on the LRS3 and TCD-TIMIT datasets, demonstrating superior performance compared to lip-to-speech baselines in perceptual speech quality and speaker similarity, while remaining competitive in automatic speech recognition (ASR).  Extensive ablation studies and cross-dataset validation are also performed.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its specific application of a conditional diffusion model with magnitude-preserving techniques to the lip-to-speech task.  While conditional diffusion models are not new in themselves, the integration of MP-ADM and the proposed MP-FiLM conditioning layer for this specific modality (audio-visual) and task presents a distinct contribution. Furthermore, pre-enhancing the "in-the-wild" audio-visual training data and pre-training with audio-only data adds to the novelty. The creation of the LRS3-CHiME3 dataset to specifically address a more difficult SNR benchmark is a useful contribution as well.

*   **Significance:** The results indicate a significant improvement in perceptual speech quality and speaker similarity over existing lip-to-speech methods. This has practical implications for situations where audio is missing or severely degraded, such as restoring archival footage, assisting individuals with hearing impairments, or enhancing communication in noisy environments.  The maintained competitiveness in ASR is important. The out-of-domain performance validates the generalization capability of the method.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined architecture and training procedure.
    *   Thorough experimental evaluation with multiple datasets and metrics.
    *   Comparison to strong baselines, including both lip-to-speech and speech enhancement methods.
    *   Ablation studies that validate design choices.
    *   Formal listening experiments that complement instrumental evaluations.
    *   Code and models made available, promoting reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While MP-FiLM is presented as a novel contribution, its novelty can be perceived as incremental. There exist other variants of feature-wise modulation. The magnitude preservation argument is reasonable, but more rigorous analysis of its impact would be beneficial.

    *   The reliance on a high-quality speaker embedding network is a potential bottleneck. While the paper addresses this to some degree with ablation, a more detailed analysis of the sensitivity of the system to the quality of the speaker embeddings would be beneficial.  For example, investigating more robust methods for obtaining speaker embeddings, or performing further ablations on how this interacts with the enrollment data, would be valuable.

    *   The societal implications section is relatively brief.  A more comprehensive discussion of the potential for misuse (e.g., deepfakes, manipulation of video evidence) is warranted.
    *   The paper lacks the analysis of how the proposed method deals with specific challenges in lip-to-speech, such as the viseme-to-phoneme ambiguity. While the paper mentions that longer temporal context helps, further analysis on the method's performance in cases that exhibit high viseme-to-phoneme ambiguity is warranted.

*   **Potential Influence:** The LipDiffuser model represents a significant step forward in lip-to-speech synthesis, and its open-source nature will likely encourage further research in this area. The incorporation of diffusion models and magnitude-preserving techniques could influence the design of future generative models for other audio-visual tasks. The creation and open-sourcing of the LRS3-CHiME3 dataset would influence future work by establishing a new baseline for performance comparison.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a well-executed application of conditional diffusion models to lip-to-speech, the architectural innovation (MP-FiLM) is somewhat incremental, and there are potential limitations regarding speaker embedding quality and specific challenges within lip-to-speech itself. However, the demonstrable performance improvements, comprehensive evaluation, and availability of code and models make this a valuable contribution. The work also opens up new avenues of research by showing how specific techniques can be effectively adapted for audio-visual synthesis. Therefore, the paper warrants a score above average.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: eess.AS
- **Score**: 7/10

### Patho-R1: A Multimodal Reinforcement Learning-Based Pathology Expert Reasoner
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11404v1)
- **Authors**: Wenchuan Zhang, Penghao Zhang, Jingru Guo, Tao Cheng, Jie Chen, Shuwan Zhang, Zhang Zhang, Yuhao Yi, Hong Bu
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in vision language models (VLMs) have enabled broad progress in the general medical field. However, pathology still remains a more challenging subdomain, with current pathology specific VLMs exhibiting limitations in both diagnostic accuracy and reasoning plausibility. Such shortcomings are largely attributable to the nature of current pathology datasets, which are primarily composed of image description pairs that lack the depth and structured diagnostic paradigms employed by real world pathologists. In this study, we leverage pathology textbooks and real world pathology experts to construct high-quality, reasoning-oriented datasets. Building on this, we introduce Patho-R1, a multimodal RL-based pathology Reasoner, trained through a three-stage pipeline: (1) continued pretraining on 3.5 million image-text pairs for knowledge infusion; (2) supervised fine-tuning on 500k high-quality Chain-of-Thought samples for reasoning incentivizing; (3) reinforcement learning using Group Relative Policy Optimization and Decoupled Clip and Dynamic sAmpling Policy Optimization strategies for multimodal reasoning quality refinement. To further assess the alignment quality of our dataset, we propose PathoCLIP, trained on the same figure-caption corpus used for continued pretraining. Comprehensive experimental results demonstrate that both PathoCLIP and Patho-R1 achieve robust performance across a wide range of pathology-related tasks, including zero-shot classification, cross-modal retrieval, Visual Question Answering, and Multiple Choice Question. Our project is available at the Patho-R1 repository: https://github.com/Wenchuan-Zhang/Patho-R1.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Patho-R1: A Multimodal Reinforcement Learning-Based Pathology Expert Reasoner":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Patho-R1, a multimodal vision-language model (VLM) specifically designed for pathology image analysis and reasoning.  The authors argue that existing VLMs struggle in the pathology domain due to the complexity of pathology data and a lack of high-quality, expert-annotated datasets. Patho-R1 is trained using a three-stage pipeline: 1) continued pre-training on a large image-text corpus for knowledge infusion; 2) supervised fine-tuning on a Chain-of-Thought dataset designed to incentivize reasoning; and 3) reinforcement learning using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) and Decoupled Clip and Dynamic Sampling Policy Optimization (DAPO) for quality refinement. The authors create a new pathology dataset and a pathology-adapted CLIP model (PathoCLIP).  Experiments show that Patho-R1 achieves strong performance on various pathology-related tasks, including zero-shot classification, cross-modal retrieval, visual question answering, and multiple-choice questions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Dataset Curation:** The creation of a high-quality, reasoning-oriented pathology dataset is a significant contribution. The emphasis on "textbook-quality" data and the incorporation of expert knowledge addresses a key limitation of existing pathology datasets. The semi-automated data curation pipeline helps ensure scalability.
*   **Multimodal RL Approach:**  The use of reinforcement learning (GRPO/DAPO) to refine the reasoning capabilities of the model is a novel and promising approach. Pathology diagnosis often requires complex reasoning, so focusing on improving this aspect is well-motivated. The adoption of reinforcement learning for model adaptation and improvement, specifically focusing on domain-aligned reward mechanisms and innovative techniques like DAPO, signifies a substantial advancement in model training methodologies.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results are compelling, demonstrating the effectiveness of Patho-R1 and PathoCLIP across a range of pathology tasks. Patho-R1 consistently outperforms other models, indicating the success of the training pipeline and the quality of the created dataset.
*   **Open Source Contribution:**  Releasing the model weights and making the project publicly available significantly benefits the pathology research community and facilitates further research and development in this area.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dataset Scalability and Generalizability:** While the dataset curation is a strength, the 500k SFT samples and 10k RL samples, while high-quality, are still limited compared to datasets used in general-purpose VLMs.  The reliance on pathology textbooks might limit the generalizability of the model to real-world clinical data, where variations and artifacts are more common. It is also unclear how representative are the textbooks employed in training.
*   **Over-Reliance on LLM-Judge:** The evaluation strategy relying on Deepseek-R1 as an LLM judge can be subjective and might introduce biases. A more objective evaluation metric, such as comparison to human expert diagnoses, would strengthen the validity of the results.
*   **Limited Exploration of Model Robustness:** While the paper shows strong performance, it could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the model's robustness to noisy data, adversarial examples, or variations in image quality, all of which are common challenges in real-world pathology.
*   **Lack of Comparative Baseline:** The evaluation doesn't present a comparative performance analysis with established state-of-the-art pathology-specific models like PLIP or CONCH, potentially undermining the assessment of the model's advancement.
*   **Limited Generalization of RL training:** Details on the implementation of reinforcement learning (GRPO/DAPO), especially the specifics of reward functions and training stability, are limited. An in-depth exploration of how various reward functions were designed, along with a comparative analysis, could provide more valuable insights into the effectiveness of RL in this context.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper demonstrates novelty in several aspects: the dataset curation pipeline, the application of RL techniques to pathology VLMs, and the creation of PathoCLIP. The significance lies in addressing a critical gap in the field by creating a model capable of more robust reasoning in pathology, which is a crucial component for clinical applications. The paper advances the state of the art in pathology VLMs. The novel application of GRPO and DAPO strategies enhances the model's ability to refine its reasoning quality in the context of multimodal data.

**Score:** 7/10

**Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of pathology VLMs through its novel dataset curation and the use of RL for improving reasoning capabilities. The empirical results are encouraging, and the open-source release is commendable. However, the limitations in dataset size/generalizability, potential biases in evaluation, and the need for more robustness analysis prevent a higher score.

The creation of a large-scale, pathology-specific multimodal corpus and the development of PathoCLIP are significant milestones. The implementation of RL methodologies, especially GRPO and DAPO, underscores a strong emphasis on enhancing the reasoning capabilities of pathology VLMs, facilitating more clinically aligned diagnostic outcomes. Although there's room for improvement regarding dataset scale, generalizability, and objectivity in evaluation, the present study significantly advances pathology VLMs and provides a solid foundation for future improvements and validations, reflecting its considerable novelty and significance in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### EmotionHallucer: Evaluating Emotion Hallucinations in Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11405v1)
- **Authors**: Bohao Xing, Xin Liu, Guoying Zhao, Chengyu Liu, Xiaolan Fu, Heikki Kälviäinen
- **Abstract**: Emotion understanding is a critical yet challenging task. Recent advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have significantly enhanced their capabilities in this area. However, MLLMs often suffer from hallucinations, generating irrelevant or nonsensical content. To the best of our knowledge, despite the importance of this issue, there has been no dedicated effort to evaluate emotion-related hallucinations in MLLMs. In this work, we introduce EmotionHallucer, the first benchmark for detecting and analyzing emotion hallucinations in MLLMs. Unlike humans, whose emotion understanding stems from the interplay of biology and social learning, MLLMs rely solely on data-driven learning and lack innate emotional instincts. Fortunately, emotion psychology provides a solid foundation of knowledge about human emotions. Building on this, we assess emotion hallucinations from two dimensions: emotion psychology knowledge and real-world multimodal perception. To support robust evaluation, we utilize an adversarial binary question-answer (QA) framework, which employs carefully crafted basic and hallucinated pairs to assess the emotion hallucination tendencies of MLLMs. By evaluating 38 LLMs and MLLMs on EmotionHallucer, we reveal that: i) most current models exhibit substantial issues with emotion hallucinations; ii) closed-source models outperform open-source ones in detecting emotion hallucinations, and reasoning capability provides additional advantages; iii) existing models perform better in emotion psychology knowledge than in multimodal emotion perception. As a byproduct, these findings inspire us to propose the PEP-MEK framework, which yields an average improvement of 9.90% in emotion hallucination detection across selected models. Resources will be available at https://github.com/xxtars/EmotionHallucer.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Emotion Hallucer: Evaluating Emotion Hallucinations in Multimodal Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EmotionHallucer, a novel benchmark designed to detect and analyze emotion-related hallucinations in multimodal large language models (MLLMs). The authors argue that while MLLMs show promise in emotion understanding, they often generate irrelevant or nonsensical content, lacking the innate emotional instincts that humans possess. EmotionHallucer assesses these hallucinations from two dimensions: emotion psychology knowledge (factuality) and real-world multimodal perception (faithfulness). The benchmark uses an adversarial binary question-answering (QA) framework, with carefully crafted basic and hallucinated QA pairs. The authors evaluate 38 LLMs and MLLMs, revealing that current models struggle with emotion hallucinations, closed-source models perform better than open-source models, and models perform better with emotion psychology knowledge than with multimodal emotion perception.  Finally, the authors propose a framework PEP-MEK, which yields improvement of 9.90% in emotion hallucination detection.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in its novelty. To the best of our knowledge, the EmotionHallucer benchmark is the first dedicated attempt to systematically evaluate emotion-related hallucinations in MLLMs.  The breakdown into emotion psychology knowledge vs. multimodal perception is also a valuable contribution, highlighting different failure modes. The adversarial QA approach and the explicit construction of hallucinated examples is also well-considered and improves the reliability of the evaluation compared to relying on model generation. The PEP-MEK framework, while perhaps not deeply groundbreaking in its architectural design, represents a practical effort to address the identified problem.

**Significance:** The work addresses an important and under-explored issue: the potential for MLLMs to generate emotionally "plausible" but incorrect or nonsensical information. This is particularly concerning given the increasing use of these models in applications that directly involve human well-being and social interaction (e.g., mental health support, educational tools). By providing a benchmark and initial results, the authors encourage further research into developing more reliable and trustworthy emotion-aware MLLMs.  The findings on the weaknesses of open-source models compared to closed-source counterparts (at least at the time of writing) and the challenges in moving from psychological knowledge to real-world perception tasks are valuable insights for the community.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Benchmark:** Introduction of EmotionHallucer fills a gap in the evaluation of MLLMs.
*   **Clear Problem Definition:** Articulates the concept of emotion hallucinations and their potential risks.
*   **Well-Designed Evaluation Framework:** The adversarial QA framework and two-dimensional assessment strategy provide a rigorous and reliable evaluation.
*   **Empirical Validation:**  Extensive evaluation of 38 models provides valuable insights into the current state of MLLM emotion understanding.
*   **Practical Solution:** The PEP-MEK framework demonstrates a potential approach to mitigate emotion hallucinations.
*   **Clearly articulated limitations:** the limitations the benchmark's focus on English only, the possibility of noise introduced by human annotations, the underexplored causes of emotional hallucinations.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Complexity of Hallucinations:** While the adversarial QA framework is robust, some of the hallucinated examples might be relatively simple to detect. More subtle, context-dependent hallucinations could pose a greater challenge.  The effectiveness of the PEP-MEK framework needs to be examined with increasingly complex scenarios and different model architectures.
*   **Potential for Annotation Bias:** Although the authors use cross-review, some degree of annotation bias may be present in creating the adversarial examples (i.e., annotators may unconsciously create examples that their own human reasoning finds obvious).  The definition of what constitutes a valid "hallucination" in an emotion context is still somewhat subjective, and inter-annotator agreement metrics could have strengthened the paper.
*   **Scope of Emotions:** The benchmark could be expanded to cover a wider range of emotions beyond the basic ones, and delve into more nuanced and complex emotional states.
*   **The description of the PEP-MEK is limited:** The explanation of the internal workings of the PEP-MEK is sparse, and a detailed breakdown of components and their configurations in the appendix is necessary for reproducibility.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to significantly influence research in the area of emotion-aware MLLMs by:

*   Providing a standardized benchmark for evaluating emotion hallucinations.
*   Motivating the development of more robust and reliable models.
*   Highlighting the need for improved training data and reasoning strategies for emotion understanding.
*   Offering insights into the limitations of current models and directions for future research.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by introducing a novel benchmark for evaluating an important and under-explored problem.  The evaluation framework is well-designed and the empirical results are informative.  However, the limitations mentioned above, including the simplicity of some hallucinations, potential annotation bias, limited emotion scope, and a lack of detailed explanation of the PEP-MEK framework, prevent it from achieving a higher score.  Overall, the paper represents a significant step forward in understanding and addressing emotion hallucinations in MLLMs, but more in-depth work is needed to address its shortcomings and explore its full potential.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Visual Planning: Let's Think Only with Images
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11409v1)
- **Authors**: Yi Xu, Chengzu Li, Han Zhou, Xingchen Wan, Caiqi Zhang, Anna Korhonen, Ivan Vulić
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) and their multimodal extensions (MLLMs) have substantially enhanced machine reasoning across diverse tasks. However, these models predominantly rely on pure text as the medium for both expressing and structuring reasoning, even when visual information is present. In this work, we argue that language may not always be the most natural or effective modality for reasoning, particularly in tasks involving spatial and geometrical information. Motivated by this, we propose a new paradigm, Visual Planning, which enables planning through purely visual representations, independent of text. In this paradigm, planning is executed via sequences of images that encode step-by-step inference in the visual domain, akin to how humans sketch or visualize future actions. We introduce a novel reinforcement learning framework, Visual Planning via Reinforcement Learning (VPRL), empowered by GRPO for post-training large vision models, leading to substantial improvements in planning in a selection of representative visual navigation tasks, FrozenLake, Maze, and MiniBehavior. Our visual planning paradigm outperforms all other planning variants that conduct reasoning in the text-only space. Our results establish Visual Planning as a viable and promising alternative to language-based reasoning, opening new avenues for tasks that benefit from intuitive, image-based inference.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Visual Planning: Let's Think Only with Images":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel paradigm called "Visual Planning" for machine reasoning.  Instead of relying on text as the primary medium for expressing and structuring reasoning (even when visual information is present), the authors propose that models plan *directly* in the visual domain.  This involves generating sequences of images that encode step-by-step inference, analogous to how humans might sketch or visualize future actions.  To implement this, they present Visual Planning via Reinforcement Learning (VPRL), a reinforcement learning framework for training large vision models (LVMs) to perform visual planning. VPRL leverages Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). The authors evaluate their approach on visual navigation tasks (FROZENLAKE, MAZE, MINIBEHAVIOR), demonstrating superior performance compared to text-based reasoning methods and improved generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strength:** The central idea of Visual Planning—reasoning entirely through visual representations without textual mediation—is novel. Current multi-modal approaches still predominantly rely on grounding visual information in text before performing reasoning. This paper directly challenges that assumption.
*   **Strength:** The VPRL framework is a novel RL method adapted for image generation. The two-stage approach (initialization through random walks followed by reinforcement learning with a progress reward) is designed specifically for this problem, leveraging state transitions for effective learning.
*   **Weakness:** While the high-level concept is novel, the individual components (RL, LVMs, image generation) are well-established. The novelty lies in how they are combined and applied to this specific reasoning task.
*   **Weakness:** The rule-based state-action parsing might seem like a naive implementation in a rapidly evolving computer vision landscape. This is a notable point against the paper's adaptability and application of modern techniques for environment interpretation and feature extraction.

**Significance:**

*   **Strength:** If successful, this paradigm shift could be significant.  For tasks that are inherently visual/spatial, reasoning directly in the image domain could lead to more efficient, accurate, and intuitive solutions. It opens up a different avenue for tackling complex planning tasks.
*   **Strength:** The empirical results, demonstrating substantial performance gains over text-based methods, provide compelling evidence for the viability of visual planning. The improved generalization reported is also significant.
*   **Weakness:** The tasks used for evaluation (FROZENLAKE, MAZE, MINIBEHAVIOR) are relatively simple, simulated environments. While these tasks are well-controlled, it is not yet clear how well the approach will scale to more complex, real-world scenarios with high-dimensional visual inputs. Also, note that these tests are well-controlled environments, and the agent is only required to solve the task; it isn't reasoning about how to solve the problem or dealing with unforeseen circumstances and nuances.
*   **Weakness:** The reliance on a simple, rule-based state-action parser is a limitation. The paper acknowledges this and points to future work using more robust CV techniques. However, the simplicity of the parser could limit the applicability of the current approach to more complex or ambiguous environments.
*   **Weakness:** The paper might oversell itself slightly on the interpretability of the approach since visualizing planning steps is not a new concept. A critical component that is missing is the "why" behind the reasoning.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to:

*   Inspire further research into visual reasoning and planning paradigms.
*   Encourage the development of more sophisticated methods for visual state representation and action parsing.
*   Shift the focus of MLLM research away from solely relying on text as the reasoning medium, especially for tasks that are predominantly visual/spatial.
*   Potentially influence the design of AI systems for robotics and other embodied agents where visual perception and action are tightly integrated.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a genuinely novel idea with promising empirical results. It challenges existing assumptions about multi-modal reasoning and proposes a new direction that could lead to significant advances in certain areas. However, the evaluation is limited to relatively simple tasks, and the implementation relies on a simple state-action parser. While the results are strong given the relative simplicity of the framework, the reliance on these highly-structured environments is concerning.

Therefore, the score reflects both the innovative nature of the core concept and the limitations of the current implementation and evaluation. It is a solid, well-executed proof-of-concept that justifies further exploration.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Is Grokking a Computational Glass Relaxation?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11411v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaotian Zhang, Yue Shang, Entao Yang, Ge Zhang
- **Abstract**: Understanding neural network's (NN) generalizability remains a central question in deep learning research. The special phenomenon of grokking, where NNs abruptly generalize long after the training performance reaches a near-perfect level, offers a unique window to investigate the underlying mechanisms of NNs' generalizability. Here we propose an interpretation for grokking by framing it as a computational glass relaxation: viewing NNs as a physical system where parameters are the degrees of freedom and train loss is the system energy, we find memorization process resembles a rapid cooling of liquid into non-equilibrium glassy state at low temperature and the later generalization is like a slow relaxation towards a more stable configuration. This mapping enables us to sample NNs' Boltzmann entropy (states of density) landscape as a function of training loss and test accuracy. Our experiments in transformers on arithmetic tasks suggests that there is NO entropy barrier in the memorization-to-generalization transition of grokking, challenging previous theory that defines grokking as a first-order phase transition. We identify a high-entropy advantage under grokking, an extension of prior work linking entropy to generalizability but much more significant. Inspired by grokking's far-from-equilibrium nature, we develop a toy optimizer WanD based on Wang-landau molecular dynamics, which can eliminate grokking without any constraints and find high-norm generalizing solutions. This provides strictly-defined counterexamples to theory attributing grokking solely to weight norm evolution towards the Goldilocks zone and also suggests new potential ways for optimizer design.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the phenomenon of "grokking" in neural networks, where a model suddenly generalizes long after achieving near-perfect training accuracy.  It proposes that grokking is analogous to a computational glass relaxation process.  The authors use concepts from statistical mechanics, specifically Boltzmann entropy, to analyze the NN's loss landscape. They find that the transition from memorization to generalization does not involve an entropy barrier, challenging previous theories suggesting it's a first-order phase transition. Instead, they identify a "high-entropy advantage under grokking."  Inspired by this, they develop a toy optimizer, WanD (Wang-Landau based optimizer), that can eliminate grokking and find high-norm generalizing solutions without regularization. The findings challenge theories attributing grokking solely to weight norm evolution and suggest new directions for optimizer design.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents several novel ideas:

*   **Glass Relaxation Analogy:** The framing of grokking as a computational glass relaxation is a conceptually interesting and potentially fruitful analogy.  While the idea of NNs as physical systems is not entirely new, the specific connection to glass physics is.
*   **Entropy Landscape Analysis:** The empirical investigation of the entropy landscape in grokking scenarios, particularly the absence of an entropy barrier, is a significant contribution that directly challenges previous claims.
*   **WanD Optimizer:** The development of an optimizer specifically designed to encourage exploration of high-entropy regions of the loss landscape is a novel approach inspired by the authors' analysis.

**Significance:** The paper's findings have the potential to significantly impact the understanding and mitigation of grokking:

*   **Challenging Existing Theories:** The rebuttal of the first-order phase transition hypothesis and the counterexamples to weight-norm-based explanations for grokking are important contributions to the ongoing debate.
*   **Optimizer Design Implications:** The success of the WanD optimizer, albeit a toy example, suggests that optimizer design could be informed by statistical-physical principles to improve generalization and avoid undesirable behaviors like grokking.
*   **Generalization Theory:** The work contributes to a deeper understanding of generalization by connecting it to the entropy landscape and highlighting the importance of exploration and thermodynamic equilibrium.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Analogy:** The paper clearly explains the connection between glass relaxation and grokking, making the ideas accessible.
*   **Rigorous Empirical Analysis:** The use of WLMD to sample the entropy landscape and the careful control of experimental conditions provide strong evidence for the authors' claims.
*   **Well-Defined Counterexamples:** The WanD optimizer offers concrete counterexamples to theories attributing grokking solely to weight decay.
*   **Comprehensive discussion and analysis of the results:** Provides reasonable justifications to conclusions reached in the paper.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Toy Optimizer:** The WanD optimizer is still a "toy" example and its practical applicability to more complex tasks is uncertain. While the proof of concept is valuable, further development and evaluation are needed.
*   **Limited Scope:** The experiments are primarily limited to modular arithmetic tasks. While grokking was originally observed in these tasks, it remains to be seen if the glass relaxation analogy holds in other domains.
*   **Simplifications in Entropy Calculation:** The definition of entropy and its practical calculation in the context of NNs involves approximations and simplifications. While these are justified, it's important to acknowledge their potential impact on the results.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper is likely to influence future research on grokking and generalization in several ways:

*   **New Theoretical Framework:** The glass relaxation analogy could provide a new framework for understanding grokking and other delayed generalization phenomena.
*   **Statistical-Physical Methods:** The paper may encourage the broader adoption of statistical-physical methods for analyzing NNs and designing better training algorithms.
*   **Optimizer Innovation:** The WanD optimizer, while still a toy, could inspire new approaches to optimizer design based on principles of exploration and thermodynamic equilibrium.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a genuinely novel perspective on grokking, supported by rigorous empirical analysis and a well-defined experimental design. While the WanD optimizer remains a proof of concept and the scope is somewhat limited, the findings challenge existing theories and suggest promising new avenues for research. The paper is well-written, and the arguments are clearly presented.  However, the impact is limited by the preliminary state of the WanD optimizer and the reliance on a specific task. Therefore, a strong but not exceptional score is warranted.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### CARES: Comprehensive Evaluation of Safety and Adversarial Robustness in Medical LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11413v1)
- **Authors**: Sijia Chen, Xiaomin Li, Mengxue Zhang, Eric Hanchen Jiang, Qingcheng Zeng, Chen-Hsiang Yu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in medical contexts, raising critical concerns about safety, alignment, and susceptibility to adversarial manipulation. While prior benchmarks assess model refusal capabilities for harmful prompts, they often lack clinical specificity, graded harmfulness levels, and coverage of jailbreak-style attacks. We introduce CARES (Clinical Adversarial Robustness and Evaluation of Safety), a benchmark for evaluating LLM safety in healthcare. CARES includes over 18,000 prompts spanning eight medical safety principles, four harm levels, and four prompting styles: direct, indirect, obfuscated, and role-play, to simulate both malicious and benign use cases. We propose a three-way response evaluation protocol (Accept, Caution, Refuse) and a fine-grained Safety Score metric to assess model behavior. Our analysis reveals that many state-of-the-art LLMs remain vulnerable to jailbreaks that subtly rephrase harmful prompts, while also over-refusing safe but atypically phrased queries. Finally, we propose a mitigation strategy using a lightweight classifier to detect jailbreak attempts and steer models toward safer behavior via reminder-based conditioning. CARES provides a rigorous framework for testing and improving medical LLM safety under adversarial and ambiguous conditions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CARES: Comprehensive Evaluation of Safety and Adversarial Robustness in Medical LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CARES, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the safety and adversarial robustness of Large Language Models (LLMs) in medical contexts. CARES addresses limitations in existing safety benchmarks by incorporating clinical specificity, graded harmfulness levels, and jailbreak-style attacks. The benchmark consists of over 18,000 prompts spanning eight medical safety principles, four harm levels, and four prompting styles (direct, indirect, obfuscated, and role-play). The authors propose a three-way response evaluation protocol (ACCEPT, CAUTION, REFUSE) and a fine-grained Safety Score metric.  Experiments with various LLMs reveal vulnerabilities to jailbreaks and over-refusal issues. A mitigation strategy using a lightweight classifier to detect jailbreak attempts is also presented.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a strong contribution by creating a medical-specific safety benchmark. While general-purpose safety benchmarks exist, CARES' focus on clinical scenarios, its inclusion of graded harm levels, and the systematic approach to jailbreak attacks significantly enhances its relevance and utility for evaluating medical LLMs. The creation of the new Safety Score metric provides a more nuanced evaluation than simple binary classification, rewarding appropriate caution. The mitigation strategy also presents a practical approach to enhancing LLM safety.
*   **Significance:** The increasing deployment of LLMs in healthcare workflows necessitates rigorous safety evaluations. CARES offers a comprehensive framework for this purpose, enabling researchers and developers to identify and address vulnerabilities in medical LLMs before real-world deployment. The benchmark's design, incorporating multiple axes of evaluation (safety principles, harm levels, prompting styles), provides a more complete picture of LLM safety behavior than existing tools. By highlighting the susceptibility of current models to jailbreaks and the tendency to over-refuse, CARES underscores the urgent need for improved safety mechanisms in this domain.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive benchmark design:** The multi-dimensional approach covers a wide range of potential safety issues in medical LLMs.
    *   **Realistic scenarios:** The benchmark incorporates clinically relevant prompts and adversarial techniques to simulate real-world usage.
    *   **Nuanced evaluation metric:** The Safety Score captures more than just refusals, rewarding appropriate caution.
    *   **Mitigation strategy:** The proposed classifier-based approach offers a practical solution for enhancing LLM safety.
    *   **Large scale:** The 18,000 prompts provided will allow for comprehensive evaluations of many different models.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **LLM-based evaluation:** Relying on GPT-4 to evaluate responses raises concerns about potential biases. While the authors manually reviewed 200 samples and found them consistent with human judgement, further validation is warranted.
    *   **Synthetic data:**  The dataset is synthetically generated, and while grounded in safety principles, may not fully capture the complexity of real-world medical discourse.
    *   **Limited jailbreak methods:** Focus on three types may not represent all adversarial techniques.

    *   **Lack of generalizability to smaller languages**: LLMs often perform worse when faced with languages other than english, so there could be other ethical safety issues that arise.
*   **Potential Influence:** CARES has the potential to become a widely adopted benchmark for evaluating medical LLM safety. It could guide the development of more robust and reliable models for healthcare applications and inform regulatory standards. The proposed mitigation strategy could be adapted and integrated into LLM development workflows.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The CARES benchmark is a significant contribution to the field of medical LLM safety. It effectively addresses existing gaps in evaluation methodologies and offers a valuable resource for researchers and developers. However, the reliance on LLM-based evaluation and the use of synthetic data are limitations that need to be acknowledged. While not without flaws, the paper's impact on the field is likely to be substantial, making a score of 8 appropriate.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### MoE-CAP: Benchmarking Cost, Accuracy and Performance of Sparse Mixture-of-Experts Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11415v1)
- **Authors**: Yinsicheng Jiang, Yao Fu, Yeqi Huang, Ping Nie, Zhan Lu, Leyang Xue, Congjie He, Man-Kit Sit, Jilong Xue, Li Dong, Ziming Miao, Dayou Du, Tairan Xu, Kai Zou, Edoardo Ponti, Luo Mai
- **Abstract**: The sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture is increasingly favored for scaling Large Language Models (LLMs) efficiently, but it depends on heterogeneous compute and memory resources. These factors jointly affect system Cost, Accuracy, and Performance (CAP), making trade-offs inevitable. Existing benchmarks often fail to capture these trade-offs accurately, complicating practical deployment decisions. To address this, we introduce MoE-CAP, a benchmark specifically designed for MoE systems. Our analysis reveals that achieving an optimal balance across CAP is difficult with current hardware; MoE systems typically optimize two of the three dimensions at the expense of the third-a dynamic we term the MoE-CAP trade-off. To visualize this, we propose the CAP Radar Diagram. We further introduce sparsity-aware performance metrics-Sparse Memory Bandwidth Utilization (S-MBU) and Sparse Model FLOPS Utilization (S-MFU)-to enable accurate performance benchmarking of MoE systems across diverse hardware platforms and deployment scenarios.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces MoE-CAP, a benchmark specifically designed to evaluate the cost, accuracy, and performance (CAP) trade-offs in sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) systems. It argues that existing benchmarks fail to accurately capture these trade-offs, leading to suboptimal deployment decisions. The paper proposes new sparsity-aware performance metrics (Sparse MBU and Sparse MFU) and a visualization method (CAP Radar Diagram) to better understand and compare MoE systems. It analyzes various MoE models and categorizes them based on their CAP optimization priorities, providing insights into their strengths and limitations. The benchmark is implemented and used to evaluate several MoE models on different hardware platforms. The paper concludes that achieving an optimal balance across CAP is difficult and highlights the need for co-designing MoE systems to align with specific applications and deployment scenarios.

**Novelty and Significance Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant and growing challenge in the deployment of MoE models. The observation that existing benchmarks are inadequate for evaluating MoE systems due to their failure to account for sparsity and heterogeneous resources is valid.

**Strengths:**

*   **Targeted Problem:** The paper tackles a crucial problem in the practical deployment of MoE models, a topic of significant interest in the LLM community.
*   **Sparsity-Aware Metrics:** The introduction of Sparse MBU and Sparse MFU is a valuable contribution. These metrics provide a more accurate assessment of memory bandwidth and compute utilization in MoE systems, addressing a key limitation of existing metrics.
*   **CAP Radar Diagram:** The CAP Radar Diagram provides a useful visualization method for comparing different MoE systems based on their trade-offs between cost, accuracy, and performance.
*   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper provides a comprehensive analysis of different MoE models and categorizes them based on their CAP optimization priorities, offering valuable insights for practitioners.
*   **Practical Implementation:** The implementation of the MoE-CAP benchmark and its use in evaluating several MoE models on different hardware platforms demonstrates the practical relevance of the proposed approach.
*   **Insights for Design:** The finding about a broader deployment possibility is an important practical aspect.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The current version of MoE-CAP focuses exclusively on inference tasks, limiting its applicability to other important deployment scenarios such as training. The insights are, therefore, more limited in generality.
*   **Benchmark Scope:** While the benchmark is automated, the range of datasets used for the evaluation is limited. The reliance on only few datasets might not expose all the edge cases and biases that could arise in real-world applications.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper introduces practical tools and findings on MoE system deployment, a very actively researched and rapidly evolving field. The sparsity-aware performance metrics and CAP radar are useful, and well-explained. However, the scope is limited as it primarily focuses on inference, and a restricted benchmark scope could also limit the generalizability of its insights. While the ideas are not deeply mathematically novel, they provide an important contribution to a current problem.

- **Score**: 7/10

### When Thinking Fails: The Pitfalls of Reasoning for Instruction-Following in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11423v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaomin Li, Zhou Yu, Zhiwei Zhang, Xupeng Chen, Ziji Zhang, Yingying Zhuang, Narayanan Sadagopan, Anurag Beniwal
- **Abstract**: Reasoning-enhanced large language models (RLLMs), whether explicitly trained for reasoning or prompted via chain-of-thought (CoT), have achieved state-of-the-art performance on many complex reasoning tasks. However, we uncover a surprising and previously overlooked phenomenon: explicit CoT reasoning can significantly degrade instruction-following accuracy. Evaluating 15 models on two benchmarks: IFEval (with simple, rule-verifiable constraints) and ComplexBench (with complex, compositional constraints), we consistently observe performance drops when CoT prompting is applied. Through large-scale case studies and an attention-based analysis, we identify common patterns where reasoning either helps (e.g., with formatting or lexical precision) or hurts (e.g., by neglecting simple constraints or introducing unnecessary content). We propose a metric, constraint attention, to quantify model focus during generation and show that CoT reasoning often diverts attention away from instruction-relevant tokens. To mitigate these effects, we introduce and evaluate four strategies: in-context learning, self-reflection, self-selective reasoning, and classifier-selective reasoning. Our results demonstrate that selective reasoning strategies, particularly classifier-selective reasoning, can substantially recover lost performance. To our knowledge, this is the first work to systematically expose reasoning-induced failures in instruction-following and offer practical mitigation strategies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "When Thinking Fails: The Pitfalls of Reasoning for Instruction-Following in LLMs":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the surprising phenomenon where Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning, typically considered beneficial for complex tasks, can degrade instruction-following accuracy in large language models (LLMs). Through extensive experiments on two benchmarks, IFEval and ComplexBench, the authors demonstrate that applying CoT prompting often leads to performance drops in instruction adherence across various models, including both general-purpose and reasoning-tuned LLMs. The paper identifies specific scenarios where reasoning either helps (e.g., formatting, lexical precision) or hurts (e.g., neglecting simple constraints, introducing redundant content).  Attention-based analysis is used to demonstrate that CoT reasoning can divert attention away from constraint-relevant tokens in the instructions. Finally, the authors propose and evaluate four mitigation strategies: in-context learning, self-reflection, self-selective reasoning, and classifier-selective reasoning, showing that selective reasoning strategies, particularly classifier-selective reasoning, can substantially recover lost performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core finding – that reasoning *harms* instruction following – is indeed novel and counterintuitive. While CoT has been extensively studied, its potential to negatively impact simpler tasks like instruction-following has been largely overlooked. The investigation into *why* this occurs, through manual analysis of failure modes and attention-based studies, adds to the novelty.

*   **Significance:** The implications of this work are significant. Instruction-following is a cornerstone of LLM usability and alignment. Demonstrating that CoT can undermine this capability raises important questions about the indiscriminate application of reasoning techniques. The proposed mitigation strategies, while not perfect, offer practical avenues for addressing the problem.  The analysis of attention patterns is particularly insightful and provides a valuable tool for understanding the inner workings of LLMs in this context.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Empirical Evaluation:** The authors conduct extensive experiments across a diverse set of LLMs and two carefully designed benchmarks. This provides strong empirical support for their claims.
    *   **Detailed Error Analysis:** The manual case studies provide a granular understanding of the different ways in which reasoning can lead to instruction violations.  These are not just statistical observations, but insightful qualitative analyses.
    *   **Attention-Based Analysis:**  The introduction of "constraint attention" and its use in visualizing and quantifying the shift in model focus during CoT reasoning is a key methodological strength.  This goes beyond simply reporting accuracy scores and attempts to explain the *mechanism* behind the phenomenon.
    *   **Practical Mitigation Strategies:** The proposed strategies offer tangible ways to improve instruction-following performance, and the comparison between them provides valuable guidance for practitioners.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Tasks:** The focus on instruction-following tasks, while important, might limit the generalizability of the findings. It's unclear whether similar reasoning-induced degradations would occur in other domains (e.g., creative writing, complex question answering with nuanced constraints). The authors acknowledge this limitation.
    *   **Mitigation Strategy Overhead:** While effective, classifier-selective reasoning requires training a separate classifier for *each* target model, adding to the computational overhead. The practicality of this approach in resource-constrained environments is questionable. Self-selective reasoning shows promise as a model-agnostic approach but could be more effective with better precision.
    *   **Over-Reliance on Heuristics and Automated Labeling for Attention Analysis:** The attention analysis could be further strengthened by including human validation to confirm automated labels on the constraints used to calculate attention scores.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence how researchers and practitioners approach reasoning in LLMs. It highlights the need for a more nuanced understanding of the trade-offs involved and encourages the development of more selective and adaptive reasoning strategies. It also opens up new avenues for research into the interpretability and control of LLM behavior.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **8**. The paper presents a novel and important finding that challenges conventional wisdom about the benefits of reasoning in LLMs. The empirical evaluation is rigorous, the error analysis is insightful, and the mitigation strategies offer practical solutions. The attention-based analysis is a key strength. However, the limited scope of the tasks and the overhead associated with some of the mitigation strategies slightly temper the overall impact. It's a highly valuable contribution that will likely stimulate further research in this area.

### MegaScale-MoE: Large-Scale Communication-Efficient Training of Mixture-of-Experts Models in Production
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.11432v1)
- **Authors**: Chao Jin, Ziheng Jiang, Zhihao Bai, Zheng Zhong, Juncai Liu, Xiang Li, Ningxin Zheng, Xi Wang, Cong Xie, Wen Heng, Yiyuan Ma, Wenlei Bao, Size Zheng, Yanghua Peng, Haibin Lin, Xuanzhe Liu, Xin Jin, Xin Liu
- **Abstract**: We present MegaScale-MoE, a production system tailored for the efficient training of large-scale mixture-of-experts (MoE) models. MoE emerges as a promising architecture to scale large language models (LLMs) to unprecedented sizes, thereby enhancing model performance. However, existing MoE training systems experience a degradation in training efficiency, exacerbated by the escalating scale of MoE models and the continuous evolution of hardware. Recognizing the pivotal role of efficient communication in enhancing MoE training, MegaScale-MoE customizes communication-efficient parallelism strategies for attention and FFNs in each MoE layer and adopts a holistic approach to overlap communication with computation at both inter- and intra-operator levels. Additionally, MegaScale-MoE applies communication compression with adjusted communication patterns to lower precision, further improving training efficiency. When training a 352B MoE model on 1,440 NVIDIA Hopper GPUs, MegaScale-MoE achieves a training throughput of 1.41M tokens/s, improving the efficiency by 1.88$\times$ compared to Megatron-LM. We share our operational experience in accelerating MoE training and hope that by offering our insights in system design, this work will motivate future research in MoE systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the MegaScale-MoE paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MegaScale-MoE, a production system for efficiently training large-scale Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models. It tackles the communication bottleneck inherent in MoE training through three main strategies: (1) customizing parallelism strategies for attention and FFN modules to reduce communication volume, (2) overlapping communication with computation at both inter- and intra-operator levels using holistic scheduling and fine-grained tiling, and (3) applying communication compression techniques like FP32-to-BF16 gradient reduction and per-token quantization for FP8 training. The system achieves a significant throughput improvement (1.88x) compared to Megatron-LM when training a 352B MoE model on 1,440 NVIDIA Hopper GPUs. The paper details the design, implementation, and operational experiences, including insights into scaling and model convergence.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper combines several existing techniques (e.g., sequence parallelism, expert parallelism, communication-computation overlap) but contributes by **judiciously selecting and customizing** them specifically for the MoE architecture and optimizing them in a production environment. While individual techniques are not entirely new, their **integrated application and detailed tuning** within a large-scale MoE training context represents a significant engineering achievement. The specific intra-operator communication and computation overlap strategies, particularly within GroupedGEMMs for experts, seem more novel. The design considerations for balancing communication and computation as hardware evolves (especially regarding the ratio *R* and the scaling discussion) add another layer of novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from its practical impact on enabling the efficient training of very large MoE models. The ability to scale MoE training effectively has direct implications for developing more powerful and cost-effective LLMs. The shared operational experience and system design insights provide valuable guidance to other researchers and practitioners in the field. The demonstration of stable convergence with FP8 and the detailed ablation studies are particularly useful contributions.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Approach:** Addresses communication bottlenecks from multiple angles (parallelism, overlapping, compression).
    *   **Production-Oriented:** Provides practical insights from real-world deployment and large-scale training runs.
    *   **Detailed Ablation Studies:** Quantifies the benefits of individual optimizations, enabling informed design decisions.
    *   **Hardware-Aware:** Takes into account the evolving capabilities of hardware and designs system optimizations accordingly.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the key concepts and techniques.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** While the paper provides insightful empirical results, it lacks a deeper theoretical analysis of the communication patterns and performance bottlenecks. More formal modeling could further refine the optimization strategies.
    *   **Dependency on Megatron-LM:** The reliance on Megatron-LM as a base makes it somewhat harder to isolate the specific contributions of MegaScale-MoE. A comparison with other frameworks (like DeepSpeed) *without* their MoE-specific optimizations would further emphasize the impact of the work.
    *   **Lack of Generalizability Discussion:** While focused on Hopper GPUs, further discussion on how the design might adapt to other architectures (e.g., AMD GPUs, custom accelerators) would strengthen the paper.

* **Potential Influence:** MegaScale-MoE is likely to significantly influence the development of future MoE training systems. The paper's insights into communication optimization, hardware-aware design, and stable FP8 training will be valuable to both researchers and practitioners. The production deployment and scalability demonstration further solidify its potential impact.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-engineered system with substantial practical value. While many individual techniques aren't entirely novel, the careful selection, customization, and integration of these techniques within a production-scale MoE training system represents a significant engineering achievement. The shared operational experience and detailed ablation studies provide actionable insights to the research community. While a deeper theoretical analysis and broader framework comparison would strengthen the paper, its practical impact and demonstrated scalability are compelling.
